8      The supplement collects general approximation arguments, capital-model proofs,                  8

9    the complete original economic applications, implementation details, and indepen- 9
10   dent diagnostics. It uses the main article’s notation and cross-references. Supple- 10
11   mentary results and equations use separate continuous sequences S.1, S.2, and so                  11

12   forth. Tables retain their appendix-letter prefixes. The final source index identifies            12

13   the exact preceding numerical record.                                                             13

3                                                                                                    3

4        The theorem replaces an assertion about a global minimizer of a composite loss              4

5    with a statement about the exact computational objects NBO estimates. It allows                 5

6    errors, does not require that a finite network class contain V ∗ , and bounds perfor-           6

7    mance against all admissible policies covered by comparison. A small sampled                    7

8    residual is not yet the uniform hypothesis of the theorem. Section C.2.4 addresses              8

9    that distinction.                                                                               9

11                   A.1. A positive-weight recursive Bellman approximation                          11

12                                                                                                   12
         Let Pha be a nonnegative transition/interpolation operator on a finite state set,
13                                                                                                   13
     with row sums one after adjoining absorbing states. Boundary values are fixed. For
14                                                                                                   14
     a continuation array w, define Ψha w pointwise as the scalar solution
15                                                                                                   15

16                        z = Pha w + h f ( x, a, z),   Th w = max Ψha w.                    (S.5)   16
                                                               a∈Ah ( x )
17                                                                                                   17

18   For stationary calculations assume f y ≤ −λ < 0 on an invariant value domain, and               18

19   require that all scalar roots remain in that domain. The map z 7→ z − h f ( x, a, z)            19

20   is strictly increasing with slope at least 1 + λh. Hence the root is unique and Ψha             20

21   is order preserving and a sup-norm contraction with factor qh = (1 + λh)−1 . The                21

22   maximum over feasible actions preserves these properties. A root bracket must                   22

23   contain the root; arbitrary clipping of utility values is not part of the definition.           23

24       For a finite horizon, the same implicit construction is used backward from the              24

25   prescribed terminal array. Under the Lipschitz condition in Assumption 1, hL < 1                25

26   gives a one-step Lipschitz constant at most (1 − hL)−1 . Discount contraction is not            26

27   assumed for a recursive parameter regime where it has not been verified. For a                  27

28   time-additive problem, the positive-weight backup hr0 + e−ρh Pha w is also consistent           28

29   and monotone. It is the backup used by the NDU reference laboratory.                            29

30                                                                                                   30

       This result applies regardless of how the neural array was obtained. Neural pro-

16     T HEOREM S.3—Viscosity-consistent neural Bellman approximation: Consider                          16

17   stationary problems satisfying comparison in a bounded continuous class and the strict              17

18   utility monotonicity used in Theorem S.2. Suppose the positive-weight schemes are stable,           18

19   locally consistent with the HJB operator, and attain the prescribed boundary data in the            19

20   limit. The feasible action approximations become dense with vanishing maximization error.           20

21   If neural arrays and feasible actors satisfy                                                        21

22                                                                                                       22
                                             δh + ϵh = o (h),                                    (S.7)
23                                                                                                       23

24                                                                                                       24
     then their interpolated values converge locally uniformly to the unique viscosity solution.
25                                                                                                       25
     Uniform convergence up to the boundary follows when boundary barriers give uniform
26                                                                                                       26
     boundary attainment. Their discrete policy-value arrays converge to the same limit. No
27                                                                                                       27
     assertion of convergence of the policy functions themselves is required.
28                                                                                                       28

29     Here stability, local consistency, comparison, and boundary attainment are                        29

30   numerical-analysis hypotheses, not consequences of calling a representation neural. 30
31   The proof is given in the appendix. A finite-horizon version uses backward error                    31

32   accumulation: terminal error tends to zero and the sum of one-step errors tends to                  32
     4

1    zero after multiplication by the one-step Lipschitz factors. This covers finite-horizon           1

2    recursive problems without imposing stationary discount contraction.                              2

3       For a diffusion with m Brownian factors, an interior semi-Lagrangian construc- 3
                                         √
4    tion uses the 2m locations x + hb ± mh σe j , each with weight 1/(2m). Positive 4
5    multilinear interpolation preserves monotonicity. For smooth test functions its inter- 5
6    polation contribution is O(∆x2 /h); thus ∆x2 /h → 0 is a sufficient interior coupling             6

7    condition. The mean and covariance of the cubature increments match those of the                  7

8    specified Brownian diffusion to first order. Stopping and reflection require consis- 8
9    tent boundary transitions. Negative interpolation weights are not permitted in this               9

10   construction.                                                                                     10

11       Appendix C gives the differential-residual counterexample, independent domain                 11

12   and action coverage, the complete-action envelope, and the guarded finite-model                   12

13   evaluation map. Appendix D proves the results, including finite-horizon error                     13

14   accumulation.                                                                                     14

16                      A.2. An operational evaluation–improvement account                             16

17       The costate in a rollout is the derivative of a specified continuation problem. Its           17

18   approximation error has to be measured where the new policy uses that continua- 18
19   tion. We now make both objects explicit. The construction uses the capital economy,               19

20   including its dense production interaction, common and idiosyncratic shocks, con- 20
21   sumption curvature, and terminal dispersion preference. It also accommodates a                    21

22   critic fitted on a coarser grid than the one used for assessment.                                 22

8    placing them by their positive parts would change the estimand. Moreover,                             8

9    Eraw = T ∥ Z1 − Z2 ∥2d /2 estimates the integrated mean-square error of one raw bank                  9

10   on this same occupation law. A confidence bound for E − Eraw therefore assesses the                   10

11   critic’s reusable costate prediction against a direct bank with the declared number                   11

12   of rollouts. It concerns this evaluation subproblem; the direct method comparison                     12

13   continues to use the returned policies’ independently assessed payoffs.                               13

14     The supplement gives explicit clipping ranges and expectation allowances for                        14

15   these statistics. They exploit the common quadratic terminal component and anti- 15
16   thetic cancellation. They do not use an empirical maximum as a population bound. 16
17   Independent, equally sized samples for every member of a declared finite stream                       17

18   distribution may be pooled using the maximum proved range and the mean numer- 18
19   ical and tail allowances. The resulting target is the uniform mean over all declared                  19

20   streams.                                                                                              20

7
         This account separates three observable economic requirements: a continuation             7

8
     that predicts a useful feasible change, an error small enough under the candidate’s           8

9
     occupation law, and a sufficiently accurate implementation. The finite Bellman                9

10
     identity is exact; the transfer of payoffs to the diffusion is quantitative. Neither          10

11
     equality of discrete and continuous costates nor global concavity of the full adapted- 11

12
     control problem is assumed.                                                                   12

                     A.3. When is state-dependent management worth its fee?                        14

15       To give the policy differences a decision interpretation, consider a proportional         15

16   resource cost τ ∈ [0, 1) for administering a state-dependent consumption rule. Gross          16

17   withdrawals from capital remain mi eYi , and the service absorbs τmi eYi . Household          17

18   consumption is (1 − τ )mi eYi . Production, gross depletion, the gross-withdrawal             18

19   adjustment cost, and terminal capital are unchanged. This is a self-financed resource         19

20   experiment rather than an externally financed utility supplement. It changes the              20

21   specified fee parameter, not the model used to train or benchmark the gross policy. 21
                  RT
22     Let A T = 0 e−ρt dt. Log felicity gives the exact identity                        22

23                                                                                                 23

24                    JPτ ( a) − JP (m0 ) = JP ( a) − JP (m0 ) + A T log(1 − τ ).         (S.17)   24

25                                                                                                 25
     Hence a positive certified improvement L a supports every fee τ satisfying
26                                                                                                 26

27                                                                                                 27
                                      τ < 1 − exp(− L a /A T ).
28                                                                                                 28

29   A negative upper endpoint after the fee rules out an improvement for that candidate           29

30   under the confidence event; an interval spanning zero is inconclusive. The fee grid is        30

31   fixed before the final simulation. We do not equate wall-clock cost to a monetary fee         31

32   or call these deliberately stylized balance sheets a calibrated household population. 32
                                            NBO SUPPLEMENT                                     9

1    The experiment instead states precisely which adoption decisions are supported by              1

2    the verified utility differences.                                                              2

4       APPENDIX B: E CONOMIC A PPLICATIONS OF THE B ELLMAN S TRUCTURE                              4

5                                                                                                   5
       The motivating economic problem combines external choices with the costly ad-
6                                                                                                   6
     justment of preferences. Recursive utility changes how a given policy is evaluated;
7                                                                                                   7
     preference adjustment adds an internal choice to its Hamiltonian; and strategic
8                                                                                                   8
     interaction changes whose deviations define improvement. These distinctions de-
9                                                                                                   9
     termine the economic interpretation of NBO. The capital economy studied below
10                                                                                                  10
     provides a setting in which policy-specific verification can be carried through to a
11                                                                                                  11
     high-dimensional diffusion. The preference, temporal-self, and game applications
12                                                                                                  12
     establish the corresponding economic objects and their own verification results.
13                                                                                                  13

14                                                                                                  14
                        B.1. Costly preference adjustment and portfolio choice
15                                                                                                  15
       Let u denote risk aversion and X financial wealth. The household chooses con-
16                                                                                                  16
     sumption c, portfolio share p, and preference-adjustment effort θ. The preference
17                                                                                                  17
     state is reflected on a declared interval [u, ū] ⊂ (1, ∞), and wealth follows the port-
18                                                                                                  18
     folio diffusion:

15     The numerical application implements these choices jointly. The finite-action                  15

16   study evaluates complete policies by independent backward recursion. The                         16

17   continuous-action study trains the actor through its own payoff and verifies its                 17

18   hybrid policy with a complete action-box enclosure. Fresh training on nested grids               18

19   separates the effect of resolution from the evaluation of a frozen coarse policy. 19
20   A model-specific compensating-consumption calculation translates its cardinal                    20

21   utility bounds into an explicit transfer experiment. Supplementary Sections I.3,                 21

22   I.3.4, and I.8 give the full model, all recorded policies, refinement evidence, and              22

23   compensation account.                                                                            23

25                           B.2. Recursive utility and temporal selves                               25

26     Recursive utility enters policy evaluation through the policy’s own continuation               26

27   utility. With a = 1 − 1/ψ, b = 1 − γ, and bV > 0, the Epstein–Zin aggregator used                27

28   here is                                                                                          28

29                                         ρ                                              29
                                f (c, V ) = {c a (bV )1−a/b − bV }.                (S.21)
30                                         a                                              30

31   The invariant utility interval and boundary payoff are part of the evaluation prob- 31
32   lem. Supplementary Section I.2 derives the homothetic policy conditions and veri- 32
                                           NBO SUPPLEMENT                                 11

1    fies the matching-bequest experiment, including the barriers that keep every policy        1

2    utility in the aggregator’s domain. This application tests recursive evaluation while      2

3    maintaining the same separation between value approximation and feasible action            3

4    improvement.                                                                               4

5      Time inconsistency requires a different improvement criterion. For the continuous        5

6    discount kernel                                                                            6

7                                                                                               7
                    h(s) = βe−ρs + (1 − β)e−(ρ+κ )s ,               0 < β ≤ 1,   κ > 0,
8                                                                                               8

9    two critics evaluate the same future policy at the two discount rates. Writing these       9

10   values as W1 , W2 , the current self uses V = βW1 + (1 − β)W2 and chooses an instan- 10
11   taneous deviation by maximizing u( a) + L a V. Supplementary Section I.4 states            11

12   the complete component equations and derives the spike-deviation condition. In             12

13   the homothetic consumption economy, the equilibrium consumption share is the               13

14   unique root in its admissible interval, and stronger present bias raises that share. 14
15   Independently optimized finite-duration deviations check the limiting equilibrium          15

16   condition. Thus the actor’s objective follows from the temporal equilibrium concept,       16

17   while the critics retain their policy-evaluation role.                                     17

18                                                                                              18

19                       B.3. Capital investment and strategic deviations                       19

20     In the dynamic Cournot application, firm i chooses investment in its own capital,        20

21   with                                                                                       21

22                                                                                              22
                         dK i = ( I i − δK i ) dt + σK K i dW i ,       qi = AK i .
23                                                                                              23

24   Its flow profit is PN (K )qi − I i − bi ( I i )2 /2, where the market-size normalization   24

25   in PN is specified for each economy. Given rivals’ policies, a critic evaluates the        25

26   resulting own-firm payoff and the actor changes only its own action. Consequently,         26

27   the relevant economic error is the gain from a complete unilateral deviation against       27

28   the frozen rival profile.                                                                  28

29     Supplementary Sections I.5 and I.5.3 specify the boundaries, investment sets,            29

30   and market normalizations, and report independent full dynamic best responses              30

31   to every principal finite neural profile. The resulting profit-unit bounds include         31

32   all dates and states of the stated finite game. They also cover randomized and             32
     12

1    history-dependent unilateral deviations against those fixed Markov rivals, by finite                                       1

2    dynamic programming. The record reports the cost of the exhaustive training oracle                                         2

3    and any nonzero one-step equilibrium defects along with the final exploitability                                           3

4    calculation.                                                                                                               4

5      These applications give the common method a precise economic interpretation. 5
6    A recursive critic evaluates a utility process; preference and capital actors allocate                                     6

7    resources and adjustment effort; a temporal actor satisfies a spike condition; and                                         7

8    a strategic actor is assessed against unilateral best responses. Each application                                          8

9    supplies the boundary conditions, approximation class, and error account required                                          9

10   by its own economic question.                                                                                              10

3                                                                                             3

4    C.0.1. Conditioning and stopping                                                         4

5                                                                                             5
       A positive, parameter-independent state weighting can precondition the critic
6                                                                                             6
     equation without changing its zero set. If that weight depends on learned quanti-
7                                                                                             7
     ties, its derivative must be included in the stated critic objective. The homothetic
8                                                                                             8
     laboratory divides the Hamiltonian by its positive coefficient and differentiates that
9                                                                                             9
     entire normalized critic residual. In the actor step the same coefficient is detached.
10                                                                                            10
     Error conversion to the original economic equation includes the scale factor.
11                                                                                            11
       A zero residual target does not make learning dynamics stationary. For the scalar
12                                                                                            12
     loss ℓ(z) = a2 z2 /2, gradient descent is stable only when 0 < ηa2 < 2. Likewise, full
13                                                                                            13
     differentiation of δ2 /2 gives δ∇δ, while a semi-gradient TD update differentiates
14                                                                                            14
     only the current-value term. The two computations must not be compared using
15                                                                                            15
     inconsistent signs or discount conventions. If f already includes −ρv, a time dis-
16                                                                                            16
     cretization must not add a second independent discount to the same continuation
17                                                                                            17
     equation.
18                                                                                            18
       Constant-step Adam is a numerical choice in the calibration laboratory, not an
19                                                                                            19
     application of a two-timescale theorem. A genuine two-timescale analysis would
20                                                                                            20
     require nonsummable and square-summable step sizes, an actor-to-critic ratio
21                                                                                            21
     tending to zero, controlled noise, bounded iterates, and suitable attracting limiting
22                                                                                            22
     dynamics (Borkar, 2024). A stationary critic gradient need not imply a zero residual,
23                                                                                            23
     and a stationary multi-agent gradient need not imply Nash optimality. The results
24                                                                                            24
     below therefore concern verified approximation errors rather than an assumed
25                                                                                            25
     universal optimizer convergence property.
26                                                                                            26

27                                                                                            27
                   C.1. Policy Evaluation and the Recursive Policy Gradient

30   ficiently differentiable for the following calculation. The terminal payoff is inde- 30
31   pendent of the policy parameter. Assume differentiation under the expectation            31

32   is justified, that the differentiated state and utility equations are integrable, and    32
     14

1    that the boundary conditions of the differentiated problem are zero. Denote the                    1

2    directional derivative of the policy value by w.                                                   2

3      Differentiating the fixed-policy equation gives                                                  3

                     0 = wt + L aϕ w + f y (t, x, aϕ , V aϕ )w + Da H aϕ [V aϕ ] Dϕ aϕ .
5                                                                                                       5

6    The operator derivative includes the dependence of both drift and covariance on                    6

7    the action. The derivative of the generator acting on the changing value is contained              7

8    in L aϕ w, while the action derivative of the generator acting on the fixed value is               8

     This formula concerns the performance derivative from the stated initial state. A
14                                                                                                      14
     domain-uniform Hamiltonian objective uses a different weighting. Such an objective
15                                                                                                      15
     is useful for approximate pointwise improvement, but it becomes a lifetime policy
16                                                                                                      16
     gradient only with the appropriate occupation and adjoint weights. Trajectories
17                                                                                                      17

18                                                                                                      18

19                                                                                                      19
     and must be analyzed separately.
20                                                                                                      20
       The critic’s residual derivative is the derivative of the entire fixed-policy equation.
21                                                                                                      21
     In particular, its level is determined by the recursive evaluation equation together
22                                                                                                      22

23                                                                                                      23

24                                                                                                      24

26                                                                                                      26
               C.2. Representation, Boundary Conditions, and Error Measurement
27                                                                                                      27

28
     C.2.1. Smooth approximation and what it establishes                                                28

29     A derivative-approximation result concerns a smooth target on a compact domain                   29

30   and a family of networks whose capacity can increase. It does not imply that a fixed               30

31   network attains zero residual, that optimization finds its best approximation, or                  31

32   that a nonsmooth target has classical derivatives everywhere. In a smooth problem,                 32
                                          NBO SUPPLEMENT                                    15

1    increasing the representation class can reduce approximation error in the value and          1

2    derivatives. In a nonsmooth problem, NBO instead evaluates a consistent positive- 2
3    weight Bellman equation, or solves an explicitly regularized equation before taking          3

4    a justified limit.                                                                           4

5      The hard-terminal architecture is v(t, x ) = G ( x ) + ( T − t) N (t, x ). It exactly en- 5
6    forces v( T, x ) = G ( x ) independently of training. A lateral Dirichlet face needs an      6

7    additional compatible lifting and vanishing factor. For a reflected state, the Neu- 7
8    mann condition is a different constraint. A loss on only interior points leaves all of       8

9    these boundary questions unanswered. The regression test evaluates the terminal              9

10   architecture at randomly chosen states and checks exact equality, not merely a small         10

11   mean terminal error.                                                                         11

12     The recursive Epstein–Zin domain is bV > 0. An exponential output ensures                  12

13   positivity, while an interval transformation can keep bV between declared positive           13

14   barriers. If bG > 0, one terminal-compatible positive representation is                      14

15                                                                                                15

16                        bv(t, x ) = exp [log(bG ( x )) + ( T − t) N (t, x )] .                  16

17                                                                                                17

18   Its terminal value is exact. It does not by itself impose a lateral exit payoff or a         18

19   two-sided barrier. These additional conditions must be built into the architecture or        19

20   measured explicitly. Clipping an invalid utility argument inside a fractional power          20

21   changes the equation and is not an acceptable hidden numerical repair.                       21

22                                                                                                22

23                                                                                                23
     C.2.2. From residual samples to a uniform bound
24                                                                                                24

25     Let r be K-Lipschitz on a domain in Rd . If every point is within distance h        V of   25

26   a validation point, the triangle inequality gives ∥r ∥∞ ≤ maxx∈V |r ( x )| + KhV . If K      26

27   is estimated from samples rather than bounded, the resulting expression is itself a          27

28   diagnostic, not a certified uniform bound.                                                   28

29     An integral bound needs more assumptions. Suppose the sampling density is at               29

30   least p0 > 0, and each ball of radius r0 or less centered in the domain intersects the       30

31   domain in volume at least c D     r d . Let M = ∥r ∥   ∞ and assume M/ (2K ) ≤ r0 . Near a   31

32   point approaching the maximum, Lipschitz continuity gives |r | ≥ M/2 on a ball of            32
     16

1    radius M/(2K ). Hence                                                                                       1

2                                                                                        !1/(d+2)                2
                                 p0 c D                                   2d+2 K d Er2

4                                                                                                                4

5    For larger M, use the capped radius r0 and the corresponding two-case inequality. 5
6    A concentration argument is additionally needed to replace the population mean by                           6

7    an empirical one. This calculation explains why a dimension-free uniform certificate                        7

8    cannot be read directly from an average training loss.                                                      8

9      The action gap is treated similarly. If an action net has covering radius h A and the                     9

10   Hamiltonian is K A -Lipschitz in the action, the unobserved supremum exceeds the                            10

11   net maximum by at most K A h A . Thus the net gap plus this term is an upper bound. 11
12   A collection of successful deviations without a covering or duality argument is only                        12

13   a lower bound on exploitability.                                                                            13

29   traction theorem. Backward propagation of the boundary values gives vi = | xi | − 1                         29

1      An explicit positive viscosity term is another possible regularization, provided       1

2    comparison, boundary convergence, and approximation errors for the regularized           2

     C.2.4. Coverage and independent maximization
8                                                                                             8

9      A verification distribution is declared independently of the actor’s occupation        9

10   measure. It includes economically relevant counterfactual states and boundary            10

11   regions, not only states visited by a current policy. If a residual r has a known        11

12   Lipschitz bound K on a compact domain and a validation set has fill distance dfill ,     12

13   then                                                                                     13

14                                                                                            14
                                    ∥r ∥∞ ≤ max |r ( x )| + Kdfill .                 (S.25)
15
                                               x ∈V                                           15

16
     The same bound can be applied to an action gap if its modulus is controlled. 16
17
     An empirical mean alone does not give this guarantee. If the sampling density            17

18
     is bounded below and the domain has a uniform interior-volume condition, a               18

19
     Lipschitz residual also admits an L2 -to-sup estimate with exponent 1/(d + 2), as        19

20
     derived in the supplement. Its dimension dependence is material.                         20

21
       An independent action solver must provide an upper bound on the unobserved             21

22
     maximization error. Trying a few alternative actions supplies only a lower bound on      22

23
     the true gap. Concavity with a duality gap, interval bounds, exhaustive enumeration      23

24
     of a finite action set, or an action net with a known Lipschitz modulus can provide      24

25
     the needed upper bound. An unverified local actor optimizer cannot certify itself.       25

1    C.2.6. Continuous-action proposals and a budget-safe Bellman enclosure                              1

2                                                                                                        2
       The learned map and its verifier have different computational roles. In the differ-
3                                                                                                        3
     ential implementation, a feasible actor supplies an action and the critic evaluates
4                                                                                                        4
     that action’s generator. In the positive-weight implementation, write
5                                                                                                        5

6          ( Tna w)i = rn,i ( a) + qn { Pn,i ( a)w + bn,i ( a)},   ( Tn w)i = sup ( Tna w)i .   (S.27)   6
                                                                              a∈Ai
7                                                                                                        7

8    Here Pn,i ( a) has nonnegative entries with sum at most one. The term b contains                    8

9    prescribed stopping payoffs, and qn > 0. Continuous controls need not enter this                    9

10   expression concavely. Reflection, interpolation cells, and stopping decisions can                   10

11   change as the action changes. A supremum, rather than an attained maximizer,                        11

12   suffices below.                                                                                     12

13     Our continuous-action implementation fits the actor by differentiating its own                    13

14   evaluated continuation payoff. It does not construct an all-action target or receive                14

15   an exact maximizing label. A bounded tanh output enforces the control box. A                        15

16   local improvement step can subsequently compare that proposal with finitely many                    16

17   continuous perturbations. Such a step is recorded as part of a hybrid policy; it is not             17

18   identified with the raw network and is not assumed globally optimal. Critic fitting                 18

19   uses the selected feasible action and the preceding continuation critic. Training ends              19

20   before any exhaustive reference or action-cover verification begins.                                20

21     For independent verification, freeze the complete policy π = (πn ) and cover each                 21

22   action box by closed subboxes. At a state and time, let ci be an outward lower bound                22

23   obtained by evaluating any feasible action using a fixed continuation upper array. 23
24   A box can be discarded only when its verified upper bound does not exceed ci + τ. 24
25   Every box not so discarded is either subdivided or retained with its upper bound. 25
26   Exhausting a time, depth, or box budget therefore enlarges the reported bound; it                   26

27   never converts an unresolved search into a success.                                                 27

28                                                                                                       28

29     P ROPOSITION S.8—Budget-safe action and policy envelopes: Suppose the terminal                    29

30   arrays satisfy L N ≤ g ≤ UN . At each time n, evaluate the fixed-policy map outward to              30

31   obtain Ln ≤ Tnπn Ln+1 . For every state, maintain a complete action cover whose box bounds          31

32   enclose Tna Un+1 for all actions in that box. Set Un,i to the maximum of the outward-rounded        32
     20

1    pruning threshold and all unresolved box upper bounds. Then, regardless of which boxes      1

2    remain unresolved,                                                                          2

3                                                                                                3
                       Ln ≤ Vnπ ≤ Vn∗ ≤ Un ,       0 ≤ Vn∗ − Vnπ ≤ Un − Ln .            (S.28)
4                                                                                                4

5    All inequalities are on the stated nodal economy. No concavity, exact training labels, or   5

6    convergence of neural optimization is required. If Un − Tn Un+1 ≤ en 1, then its optimal- 6
7    value excess is at most                                                                     7

8                                     !                              !                           8
                               N −1                    N −1   k −1
9                              ∏ qj       ∥U N − g ∥ ∞ + ∑    ∏ qj       ek .                    9
                               j=n                     k=n    j=n
10                                                                                               10

11     The proof, including stopping faces, is in the supplement. A stored unresolved            11

12   bracket supplies an upper bound for en ; it is not replaced by the requested tolerance. 12
13   The policy lower envelope and the Bellman upper envelope are computed separately. 13
14   The latter may be shared across several frozen policies on the same economy, while          14

15   each policy requires its own evaluation. This separation prevents an expensive              15

16   verification oracle from becoming undisclosed actor supervision.                            16

17     In the preference application, the continuous box has three coordinates and the           17

18   state grid has two. Bilinear interpolation is enclosed cell by cell. On each rectangle,     18

19   its extrema occur at cell corners; a second implementation uses cell-slope enclo- 19
20   sures. If a box crosses a wealth stopping face, the verifier takes the union of the         20

21   interior continuation and the prescribed exit payoff. Their numerical values need           21

22   not coincide at off-grid exit locations. Treating the entire action objective as globally   22

23   differentiable at this switch would invalidate a derivative-only bound. Reflection          23

24   is handled by including every crossed fold. Positive consumption and a prefer- 24
25   ence interval strictly above one keep the powers and denominators in their stated           25

26   domains.                                                                                    26

27     The complete-action bound is not a continuous-state or continuous-time theorem. 27
28   The verifier interprets stored primitive coefficients and nodal abscissae as exact          28

29   real inputs and encloses subsequent operations outward. Training and verification           29

30   agree to numerical precision on the inherited action grid, but the certificate’s precise    30

31   object is this declared nodal economy, not an unspecified sequence of floating-point        31

32   evaluations. The supplemental transfer result states separately the state interpo- 32
                                          NBO SUPPLEMENT                                          21

1    lation, diffusion approximation, boundary monitoring, and action errors needed                     1

2    to recover the continuous economy. None is inferred from a sampled residual or                     2

3    silently assigned zero.                                                                            3

     D.0.1. Proof of Theorem S.1
7                                                                                                       7
       Extend the aggregator Lipschitz-continuously outside its invariant interval for
8                                                                                                       8
     this comparison argument. The extension leaves all policy utilities and the candi-
9                                                                                                       9
     date residual unchanged. For a nonnegative number c, define dc (t) = Be L(T −t) +
10                                                                                                      10
     cA L ( T − t). Then dc ( T ) = B, dc (t) ≥ B, and −d′c = Ldc + c. Since a spatially constant
11                                                                                                      11
     shift leaves the generator unchanged and f is L-Lipschitz in utility,
12                                                                                                      12

13
     F [v + dc ] ≥ F [v] − d′c − Ldc = F [v] + c,      F [v − dc ] ≤ F [v] + d′c + Ldc = F [v] − c.     13

14                                                                                                      14

15   Because F [v] = r a [v] − g a [v] ∈ [−e − η, e], v + d   e+η is a supersolution and v − de+η       15

16   a subsolution. They dominate the required stopping data on every boundary face. 16
17   A spatially constant shift also preserves an exactly satisfied homogeneous reflected               17

18   boundary condition. Comparison proves (S.3).                                                       18

19     Apply the same argument to the fixed-policy operator F a [v] = −v          t   − H a [v], with   19

20   c = e, to obtain (S.2). The triangle inequality gives (S.4), and policy admissibility              20

21   gives its nonnegative lower bound. The construction uses L ≥ 0 and dc (t) ≥ B                      21

22   specifically to cover lateral stopping boundaries; it does not incorrectly discount                22

23   their errors as if every path stopped only at T. For nonsmooth candidates the same                 23

24   argument applies when their residual inequalities are verified in the viscosity sense. 24
25   The theorem’s differential implementation, however, requires the stated smooth                     25

26   candidate.                                                                               Q.E.D.    26

27                                                                                                      27
     D.0.2. Exact policy improvement
28                                                                                                      28

29     Suppose v = V a solves the fixed-policy problem and a feasible ã satisfies                      29

30   H ã [v] ≥ H a [v] pointwise. Then F ã [v] ≤ 0 with the same boundary payoff. Policy              30

31   comparison gives V ã ≥ V a . This is a policy-space statement. A single gradient step             31

32   on a domain-averaged actor loss does not automatically meet its pointwise premise. 32
     22

1    The measured action gap and policy-evaluation errors are retained precisely because          1

2    approximate neural improvement can violate that premise.                                     2

3                                                                                                 3
     D.0.3. Proof of Theorem S.2
4                                                                                                 4

5      For a fixed state and action, let z and z̃ solve (S.5) for w and w̃. Strict monotonicity   5

6    of z − h f (z) gives                                                                         6

7                                | Pha (w − w̃)|                                                  7
                                |z − z̃| ≤       ≤ qh ∥w − w̃∥∞ .
8                                     1 + λh                                                      8

9
     The same monotonicity implies order preservation. Taking the maximum over                    9

10
     actions preserves both order and the Lipschitz constant. Banach’s fixed-point argu- 10
11
     ment supplies the two fixed points on the invariant complete value domain.                   11

12

     D.0.4. Proof of Theorem S.3
17                                                                                                17
       First, (S.6) gives
18                                                                                                18

                            ∥vh − v∗h ∥∞ ≤           =                −→ 0.                       19

20
                                             1 − qh       λ      h                                20

21   It remains to identify the limit of the exact monotone scheme. Stability makes the up- 21
22   per and lower half-relaxed limits finite. At a strict local maximum of the upper limit       22

23   minus a smooth test function, select nearby maximizing grid points. Monotonicity             23

24   compares the neighboring scheme values to that test function plus the vanishing              24

25   contact offset. Local consistency then gives the viscosity subsolution inequality. 25
26   The corresponding minima give the supersolution inequality for the lower limit. 26
27   Consistent boundary data and the assumed barriers give the required boundary                 27

28   inequalities. Comparison orders the upper limit below the lower limit. The reverse           28

29   order follows by definition, so the two limits coincide with the unique continuous           29

30   viscosity solution. The vanishing distance between vh and v∗h transfers that conver- 30
31   gence to the neural arrays. The discrete policy-value conclusion follows from the            31

32   third bound. Interpreting a discrete policy as a continuous-time implementation              32
                                           NBO SUPPLEMENT                                      23

1    additionally requires convergence of its controlled interpolation and payoff; the               1

2    theorem does not infer this merely from convergence of its state arrays. This is the            2

3    usual monotone-scheme selection argument, with the neural approximation error                   3

4    made explicit; see Barles and Souganidis (1991), Crandall et al. (1992).               Q.E.D.   4

                          D.1. Proofs for the Guarded and Signed Map
5                                                                                                5

     D.1.1. Proof of Proposition S.18                                                            6

7      At a replaced node the output equals the computed target. At any other node its           7

8    distance from that target is at most τh . The triangle inequality gives the one-step        8

9    conclusion. For a frozen policy sequence, let en be the sup-norm difference between         9

10   the guarded and exact policy values at date n. Positivity of the transition weights         10

11   and their mass bound imply en ≤ τh + ηh + qh en+1 , with e N = 0 for exact terminal         11

12   data. Backward induction gives the geometric sum. As N = T/h and qh ≤ 1, the                12

13   bound is at most T (0.1h + ηh /h) under the stated schedule. No property of the             13

14   optimizer was used. The assertion concerns the guarded nodal values. An off-node            14

15   neural interpolant, an improved policy, and a diffusion approximation each require          15

16   their separate error account. In fixed-precision arithmetic, ηh = o (h) is a maintained     16

17   refinement condition, not a fact that can be inferred as h tends to zero indefinitely.□     17

18                                                                                               18

19   D.1.2. Proof of Theorem S.19                                                                19

20                                                                                               20
       On a regular box, the payoff restricted to a line segment is continuous and piece-
21                                                                                               21
     wise continuously differentiable, with finitely many interpolation and reflection
22                                                                                               22
     knots, or is the limit of such segments. Integration of its almost-everywhere deriva-
23                                                                                               23
     tive yields
24                                                                                               24
                                          Z 1
25                                                                                               25
                     Qw ( a) − Qw (c) =         ∇ Qw (c + s( a − c)) · ( a − c) ds.
26                                         0                                                     26

27   Every partial derivative is included in its interval hull. Maximizing the resulting         27

28   interval products proves (S.110). Taking the minimum with another valid upper               28

29   enclosure preserves validity. If a partial derivative is strictly positive throughout the   29

30   box, integration along that coordinate shows that replacing it by its upper endpoint        30

31   cannot lower the payoff; the negative case is analogous. Repeating the argument             31

32   proves face domination and the dimension statement.                                         32
                                       NBO SUPPLEMENT                                   25

1      A subdivision replaces a parent by children whose union contains it. A face            1

2    reduction replaces a set by a subset with the same supremum. Removal occurs              2

3    only after that supremum has a bound no larger than the incumbent plus tolerance. 3
4    Feasible point evaluations can only increase the incumbent, so an earlier removed        4

5    box stays bounded by the final incumbent plus tolerance. The remaining boxes             5

6    keep their upper bounds without any success inference from an interrupted search. 6
7    Induction over all operations proves (S.111). At a stopping switch the line-segment      7

8    continuity premise can fail; the implementation therefore uses the natural union         8

9    enclosure and does not invoke the derivative argument there.                             9

10     For the local quadratic observation, a K-Lipschitz gradient with a zero-gradient       10

11   point in the box has norm at most Ks throughout a box of diameter s. Integration         11

12   over a segment of length at most s bounds the variation by Ks2 . This conditional        12

13   observation does not extend to an arbitrary switching box, nor does it assert that all   13

14   nodes possess such a box.                                                           □    14

     D.1.4. Proof of Proposition S.20                                                            5

6      Let m = d−1 ∑i mi . Jensen’s inequality gives d−1 ∑i log mi ≤ log m. Moreover,            6

7    d−1 ∑i tanh(( BY )i ) ≤ 1. The mean-state drift is consequently no larger than b+ − m. 7
8    The dispersion penalty at the terminal date is nonnegative and can be dropped               8

9    for an upper bound. Fubini’s theorem applied to the integrated expected mean                9

10   state and terminal mean state yields the kernel w(t). The Brownian terms have               10

11   zero mean; bounded controls and bounded nonlinear drift ensure the required                 11

12   integrability. Thus every admissible policy has expected payoff at most                     12

13                   Z T                                                                         13

14                         e          max {log m − ζm /2 + w(t)(b+ − m)} dt.                     14

16   The integrand is strictly concave in m. Its derivative is 1/m − ζm − w(t), whose            16

17   positive root gives m0 (t) before clipping. This proves the upper bound.                    17

18     For the feasible common schedule, the expected mean-state drift is at least               18

19   b− − m0 (t). Define the normalized dispersion norm ∥ Z ∥22,d = E[d−1 ∑i Zi2 ]. Cen- 19
20   tred common controls and common noise vanish from the dispersion equation. For              20

21   numbers in [−1, 1], their cross-sectional variance is at most one. Hence Minkowski’s        21

22   inequality bounds the norm of the integrated centred nonlinear drift by κT. The 22
                                                    p
23   centred idiosyncratic Brownian part has norm σI (1 − 1/d) T. A second applica- 23
24   tion of Minkowski and squaring give the upper bound Dd on expected terminal                 24

28                                                                                               28
     D.1.5. Compensation and arithmetic scope
29                                                                                               29

30     For each realized state and positive consumption, CRRA felicity is increasing             30

31   in consumption, including when u > 1 and its level is negative. The financed sup- 31
32   plement leaves the state law and every other payoff term unchanged. Its expected            32
                                       NBO SUPPLEMENT                                   27

1    payoff is therefore nondecreasing in e. Positivity of the nodal transition operator      1

2    propagates a lower interval payoff at each date. If its lower endpoint exceeds the       2

3    optimal upper envelope at every active starting node and date, the corresponding         3

4    financed supplement compensates the loss throughout that nodal economy. Bisec- 4
5    tion searches for a sufficient supplement; only the final verified inequality provides   5

6    the stated upper bound.                                                                  6

7      All implemented interval conclusions use the inherited binary64 execution con- 7
8    tract, with outward elementary operations and separately bounded transcendental          8

9    evaluations. They concern exact real interpretations of stored primitive coefficients    9

10   and grid nodes. They are not a formal verification of the interpreter, hardware,         10

11   or all library internals. In the capital bound, square-root seeds are accepted only      11

12   after interval squaring verifies both endpoint inequalities, and the time integral is    12

13   bounded by interval rectangles rather than by an adaptive quadrature tolerance. 13
14   Random stress tests are regression evidence, not substitutes for these inclusion         14

15   arguments.                                                                               15

     D.2.2. Cancellation, clipping and finite-population inference
11                                                                                              11

12     For each candidate, the exact payoff identity used in Proposition S.11 consists of       12

13   a policy production term, its consumption term, its terminal dispersion, and the           13

14   corresponding reference terms. When two candidates use the identical reference             14

15   path, subtraction cancels every exact reference term. Comparing the two policy             15

16   terms with their numerical approximations therefore requires the two policy transfer       16

17   errors and the statistic-roundoff accounts, but no reference-state transfer error. The     17

18   implementation retains conservative original statistic budgets even where their            18

19   reference quadrature components also cancel.                                               19

20     For any real x, y and positive thresholds ba , bb ,                                      20

21                                                                                              21

22                                                                                              22
                       (| x − y| − ba − bb )+ ≤ (| x | − ba )+ + (|y| − bb )+ .
23                                                                                              23

24                                                                                              24

25   Taking expectations proves the summed clipping-tail allowance without requiring            25

26   independence between policies. Applying the empirical Bernstein inequality to the          26

27   clipped difference and then to its negative gives the two one-sided bounds. A union        27

28   bound covers all declared tests. Our allocation counts both directions and every           28

29   method, profile design and auxiliary evaluation; it does not spend new confidence          29

30   mass for deterministic transformations such as (S.17).                                     30

31     For the finite population, draw I with the fixed probabilities p j , independently       31

32   of each future innovation path. The pair ( I, W ) is then independent across paths. 32
                                          NBO SUPPLEMENT                                 29

1    Conditional transfer gives                                                                1

2                                                                                              2

3                     E[ Xa,h ] − ∑ p j { Jy j ( a) − Jy j (m0 )} ≤ ∑ p j Ea,h (y j ).         3
                                    j                                 j
4                                                                                              4

5    The clipping threshold is the maximum of the profile-specific thresholds; its             5

6    expectation-tail allowance is bounded by the maximum of their allowances. The             6

7    prescribed implementation uses equal probabilities over nine fixed profiles. Both         7

8    averaging and the final subtraction are interval-enclosed. Random empirical pro- 8
9    file frequencies are not substituted for the population weights when calculating          9

10   deterministic error budgets. They are part of the sampling variation of the final         10

11   statistic.                                                                                11

12     For the paired method difference, the same profile is assigned to both policies on      12

13   each path. Common random numbers across different methods or training seeds               13

14   do not invalidate a finite-family union bound. They also do not create independent        14

15   replications of the optimizer. The ten fixed training streams are reported individually   15

16   and descriptively; no confidence assertion about an unspecified distribution of           16

17   future hyperparameter choices is inferred.                                                17

     D.2.3. Observed-history implementation
19                                                                                             19

20     Under the observation assumptions, M is a continuous martingale with bracket            20

21   ⟨ M⟩t = Qt. Let A = Σ⊤ Q−1 . The matrix AΣ is the orthogonal projector onto the row       21

22   space of Σ, and nn⊤ is the projector onto its orthogonal complement. Independence         22

23   of the private Brownian motion gives                                                      23

24                                                                                             24
                          ⟨ AM + nB0 ⟩t = ( AQA⊤ + nn⊤ )t = Id+1 t.
25                                                                                             25

26   The multivariate Lévy characterization proves the Brownian assertion. Also ΣA = Id        26

27   and Σn = 0, so ΣW e = M. On each decision cell, the action is determined at its left      27

28   endpoint by the stored internal state and past recovered increments. With that held       28

29   action, the physical state equation has a unique strong solution because the produc- 29
30   tion drift is globally Lipschitz and bounded. Induction over the cells establishes        30

31   uniqueness in law for the joint physical and internal process. Driven by the Brown- 31
32   ian motion just constructed, it therefore has the same law as the controller originally   32
     30

1    defined using directly supplied innovations. Applying the specified componentwise                 1

2    clipping to the recovered vector preserves this distributional equivalence.                       2

3      The proposition uses only private randomization and observed state history; it                  3

4    does not reveal the original null-space economic driver. Randomized controls are                  4

5    admissible in the enlarged filtration. Since the original deterministic performance               5

6    envelope holds against all such adapted controls and independent randomization                    6

7    cannot improve an unconstrained expected-payoff supremum beyond the best                          7

8    deterministic randomization realization, the comparison with the original optimum                 8

9    is preserved. The result is not an implementability theorem for discrete noisy state              9

10   measurements. The exact drift integral is stated explicitly, and errors in approximat- 10
11   ing it must be treated separately.                                                                11

11                                                                                                           11
     The first sample average is not constrained to be positive. Both identities refer to
12                                                                                                           12
     the actual held-out state distribution. They estimate the quantities in (S.29) only
13                                                                                                           13
     when that distribution is the candidate’s discounted state occupation law, or when
14                                                                                                           14
     a justified change-of-measure account is supplied. Nested-grid differences diagnose
15                                                                                                           15
     time-step sensitivity; without a remainder bound they do not bound B a,h .
16                                                                                                           16

17                                                                                                           17
       A continuous-time costate error account. There is also a direct sufficient condition
18                                                                                                           18
     for controlling the true costate, independent of a rollout bias estimate. For a smooth
19                                                                                                           19
     candidate value v, define its fixed-policy residual and terminal error by
20                                                                                                           20

21                    r [v] = −vt − L v − u(·, π ) + ρv,
                       π                     π
                                                                        bv = v( T, ·) − g.                   21

22                                                                                                           22
     Suppose bπ = c1 + f − π is continuously differentiable with ∥ Dy bπ ∥2 ≤ Lb , and
23                                                                                                           23
     suppose differentiating the following Feynman–Kac expectations is justified. If
24                                                                                                           24
     ∥dDy bv ∥d ≤ CT and ∥dDy r π [v](s, ·)∥d ≤ Cr (s) everywhere, then
25                                                                                                           25
                                                                  Z T

           ∥dDy v(t, y) − qπ (t, y)∥d ≤ e( Lb −ρ)(T −t) CT +            e( Lb −ρ)(s−t) Cr (s) ds.   (S.31)
27                                                                  t                                        27

28   To see this, write v − V π as the expected discounted terminal error plus the expected                  28

29   discounted residual integral. The state Jacobian Jt,s satisfies J̇t,s = Dy bπ (s, Ys ) Jt,s ,           29

30   Jt,t = I, and hence ∥ Jt,s ∥2 ≤ e Lb (s−t) . Differentiate the expectation and apply that               30

31   bound. A hard terminal condition gives CT = 0. Equation (S.31) is a sufficient                          31

32   mathematical account for a smooth continuous policy. A sampled residual, a rollout                      32
     34

1    grid difference, or the derivatives of a different deployed implementation do not                      1

2    supply its global hypotheses.                                                                          2

6                                                                                                      6

7    These bounds hold for every initial mean, every initial state with the stated dispersion bound,   7

8    and arbitrary adapted competitors in the original action box. They require no neural training     8

9    convergence or discretization error estimate.                                                     9

10                                                                                                     10

11     The proof uses synchronous state comparison, an exact discounted mean-state                     11

12   identity, and completion of a quadratic bound on an arbitrary policy deviation. 12
13   The complete proof appears in Section E.2. The result is a model-specific numer- 13
14   ical principle: curvature limits the benefit from changing consumption, whereas                   14

15   a spectral bound limits the propagation of that change through dense nonlinear                    15

16   production. Their balance yields a scalar integral certificate for a high-dimensional             16

17   policy. Bounded nonlinearity, the additive diffusion structure, and the chosen felicity           17

18   are essential assumptions, rather than generic properties of all HJB equations.                   18

19                                                                                                     19
     E.1.1. Computable scope and cost
20                                                                                                     20

21     An SVD is used only to propose a scalar λ. Positive pivots in an outward in- 21
22   terval LDL⊤ calculation for λI − B⊤ B certify the spectral majorant. A failed pivot               22

23   triggers enlargement, not acceptance of the SVD output. The remaining integrals                   23

24   use outward rectangle sums, polynomial exponential and logarithm bounds, and                      24

25   square-root endpoints checked by interval squaring. For a fixed majorant with                     25

26   verified pivots, this certificate requires O(d3 ) arithmetic operations and O(d2 ) stor- 26
27   age for the matrix check, followed by O( N ) scalar quadrature work. This is not a                27

28   dimension-independent training theorem or a worst-case bound on the number of                     28

29   attempted majorants.                                                                              29

30     The guarantee holds already at initialization. Training is intended to improve the              30

31   feasible policy inside the tube, not to manufacture its certificate. The anchor itself            31

32   has the tighter bound Gd and requires no training. A comparison omitting it would                 32
     36

1    overstate the role of the actor. Neither a small Gd + Dd,ϵ nor faster actor evaluation       1

2    proves a net economic advantage over that benchmark.                                         2

3                                                                                                 3

6    important: the spectral norm of B remains the ordinary Euclidean operator norm,              6

7    while the stochastic moment of cross-sectional dispersion is of order one rather than        7

8    order d.                                                                                     8

     E.2.4. Interval spectral and deployment checks                                                          13

14     For the stored exact-real matrix B, outward products and sums enclose each entry                      14

15   of B⊤ B. The Schur complement recurrence for an LDL⊤ factorization is enclosed                          15

16   entry by entry. If every pivot interval has strictly positive lower endpoint, every                     16
                                                                               √
17   exact pivot is positive, so λI − B⊤ B is positive definite. Hence ∥ B∥2 < λ. The                        17

18   proposed numerical singular value is not part of this proof; an unsuccessful interval                   18

19   factorization supplies no spectral bound.                                                               19

20     If ℓ ≤ m0 (t) ≤ u, then up(u − ϵ) ≥ m0 (t) − ϵ and down(ℓ + ϵ) ≤ m0 (t) + ϵ. Every                    20

21   finite output after projection onto (15) therefore meets the theorem’s tube condition                   21

22   under the declared arithmetic contract, regardless of errors in the neural proposal. 22
23   The proof does not verify the interpreter or processor. The arithmetic assump- 23
24   tions, overflow checks, saved weights, and input-clock convention remain explicit                       24

25   components of the computational claim.                                                                  25
