26   the controller’s internal update, not a replacement of the economic innovation law. 26
27     The scalar weights Ak , Bk are integrated from their exponential expressions. Ck ,            27

28   the reference action integral, and the within-cell production weight are enclosed on            28

29   a dyadic subdivision with 64 panels per cell. Their midpoint errors are explicitly              29

30   accumulated. If δM bounds the sum of the absolute errors in the integrated reference            30

31   actions, e βT δ      M enlarges the reference state error. The errors in Ak , Bk , Ck are multi- 31
32   plied by global bounds on consumption, its logarithm, and bounded production,                   32
                                        NBO SUPPLEMENT                                   43

1    and are added to the statistic error. These terms are not inferred from a difference      1

2    between two grids.                                                                        2

3                                                                                              3

4    E.5.3. Arithmetic contract and forward-error budget                                       4

5                                                                                              5
       All stored binary64 model coefficients denote exact real inputs to the economic
6                                                                                              6
     problem. The computation assumes round-to-nearest elementary arithmetic, the
7                                                                                              7
     usual dot-product forward-error bound, and correctly rounded input conversion. It
8                                                                                              8
     does not assume correct rounding of a platform exponential, logarithm, or hyper-
9                                                                                              9
     bolic tangent when proving a payoff endpoint. Network library functions produce
10                                                                                             10
     proposals only; the interval schedule and inward projection enforce feasibility
11                                                                                             11
     independently of proposal accuracy. The simulation drift and log utility use the
12                                                                                             12
     polynomial kernels described next. The Python interpreter, numerical libraries, com-
13                                                                                             13
     piler, hardware, and random-number generator are not machine-formally verified.
14                                                                                             14
       For | x | ≤ 20, write r = x/256 and approximate er by its degree-14 Taylor poly-
15                                                                                             15
     nomial in Horner form. Nine squarings approximate e2x . The Taylor remainder
16                                                                                             16
     is bounded by e|r| |r |15 /15!. The coefficient representation and Horner operations
17                                                                                             17
     satisfy the standard γ30 forward bound, with γ j = ju/(1 − ju) and u = 2−52 , a
18                                                                                             18
     conservative choice relative to binary64 unit roundoff. Propagating relative error
19                                                                                             19
     through nine squarings bounds the exponential relative error by less than 2−35 on
20                                                                                             20
     this range. The derivative of 1 − 2/(1 + z) with respect to log z is 2z/(1 + z)2 ≤ 1/2.
21                                                                                             21
     Including the final arithmetic and saturation outside [−20, 20] therefore bounds
22                                                                                             22
     absolute tanh error by 2−34 . The saturation error itself is below 2e−40 .
23                                                                                             23
       For positive consumption, binary exponent extraction writes x = 2e−1 (2m), with
24                                                                                             24
     m ∈ [1/2, 1). Then
25                                                                                             25

                           ∞
26                             q2j+1                            2m − 1                         26
                log x = 2 ∑           + (e − 1) log 2,     q=          ∈ [0, 1/3].
27
                          j =0
                               2j + 1                           2m + 1                         27

28                                                                                             28

29   The implementation retains 18 terms and evaluates log 2 with the same series at           29

30   q = 1/3. The geometric remainder and Horner forward error, including the bounded          30

31   exponents on the primitive consumption interval, are below 2−34 . No empirical            31

32   comparison with the platform logarithm serves as this error bound. The optional           32
     44

1    compiled kernels use identical binary64 coefficients, operation order, and disabled         1

2    fast-math transformations; the array implementation is retained as a fallback.              2

3      Clipped innovations yield a deterministic internal state cap. A conservative cap          3

4    for the implemented ranges is                                                               4

5                                                           √                                    5

6
                       Scap = 2{∥y∥∞ + Mϵ T + (σI + σC )10T/ h + 1}.                             6

7                                                                                                7
     Using γd+2 Scap maxi ∑ j | Bij | for the matrix-product error, the code propagates drift,
8                                                                                                8
     innovation-combination, and state-addition errors through the same discrete Gron-
9                                                                                                9
     wall bound. It verifies the bootstrap condition that this enlargement is less than
10                                                                                               10
     one. This condition, together with the doubled cap, bounds the entire numerical
11                                                                                               11
     path. Separately, it accumulates the errors of all statistic sums, log evaluations,
12                                                                                               12
     and terminal squares. The exact formulas and intermediate constants are returned
13                                                                                               13
     by constants; each final record saves the resulting state cap, forward-roundoff
14                                                                                               14
     bound, scalar-integration bound, and total bias endpoint. Scalar transcendental
15                                                                                               15
     evaluations used only to assemble conservative budgets receive additional 10−12
16                                                                                               16
     bias and 10−10 clipping cushions on the declared bounded parameter range; they
17                                                                                               17
     do not replace the enclosed spectral or integral calculations.
18                                                                                               18
       The sampling contract is ideal independent standard-normal innovations
19                                                                                               19
     rounded to binary64 before the specified clipping, with the forward-error allowance
20                                                                                               20
     for their conversion. Pseudorandom draws are a reproducible implementation of
21                                                                                               21
     this contract, not a proof of ideal independence. The resulting certificate is condi-
22                                                                                               22
     tional numerical mathematics and conditional statistical inference. It must not be
23                                                                                               23
     described as a machine-formal guarantee of a particular hardware random stream.
24                                                                                               24

1    E.5.5. Finite families, selection, and interpretation                                     1

2
       Conditioning on the fitted weights and their validation selection makes each final- 2
3                                                                                              3
     test statistic a fixed function of one fresh path. Each row is therefore an independent
4
     sample across paths, even when its Brownian bank is shared with other policies. 4
5                                                                                              5
     Applying Maurer and Pontil (2009, Theorem 4) to both signs and taking a union
6                                                                                              6
     bound proves the simultaneous statement. The implementation allocates 2,000
7                                                                                              7
     one-sided statements, so at most 1,000 two-sided rows may be reported under this
8                                                                                              8
     allocation. The audit rejects an overrun. It also checks source weights, path counts,
9                                                                                              9
     raw-array hashes, every recomputed mean and variance, and all displayed table
10                                                                                             10
     inputs.
11                                                                                             11
       A certificate may fail to establish improvement. That is distinct from an exception
12
     in the numerical code. Both outcomes are retained. A zero correction, a deliber- 12
13                                                                                             13
     ately saturated poor correction, and trained corrections are compared under the
14                                                                                             14
     same accounting. Small training loss or successful completion is never substituted
15
     for a positive lower improvement endpoint. If a candidate is chosen after inspect- 15
16                                                                                             16
     ing several final rows, the simultaneous guarantee continues to hold only within
17                                                                                             17
     the predeclared finite family; an undeclared search requires a new allocation and
18                                                                                             18
     independent data.

30                                                                                                 30
       Conditional independence of the two continuation banks gives
31                                                                                                 31

32                          E[ E | K, ZK , U ] = T ∥qbK ( XK,U ) − qπ          2

1    and the usual two-replicate identity gives                                                 1

3                                                                                               3

4                                                                                               4
     Uniform sampling of K proves all the mean identities in (S.15). Equal independent
5                                                                                               5
     samples from each declared stream identify the finite uniform mean. Independence
6                                                                                               6
     across streams and the nonidentical-variable version of the empirical Bernstein
7                                                                                               7
     inequality, Maurer and Pontil (2009, Theorem 11), justify pooling. Dependence
8                                                                                               8
     among M, A, E, D on one path does not invalidate a union bound across their
9                                                                                               9
     confidence statements. Clipping changes each expectation by at most its proved tail
10                                                                                              10
     allowance.
11                                                                                              11
       The finite-grid identities above concern exact real transition and reward maps
12                                                                                              12
     with the disclosed coefficients. A protected numerical audit must additionally en-
13                                                                                              13
     close its scalar weights, forward-state error, neural values and derivatives, adjoint
14                                                                                              14
     recurrence, and statistic arithmetic. The controller’s rounded actions are held fixed
15                                                                                              15
     when its rounded state is compared with the exact internal state, so this state ac-
16                                                                                              16
     count does not require a derivative of the actor. For example, if the action bridge has
17                                                                                              17

17               F.6. A signed occupation account with shared continuation paths                    17

18     Fix the finite economy in (S.8)–(S.9) and condition on the fitted candidate, the             18

19   continuation predictor, and their selection. Let Fk ( x; ξ ) be the reference continuation     19

20   payoff along one future innovation array, starting at the postdecision mean x,                 20

21   including the innovation at k, excluding the current reward, and using the original            21

22   global grid thereafter. Thus EFk ( x; ξ ) = Skπ ( x ). The two endpoint rollouts below         22

23   use the same array ξ. For r independent arrays, average each array and its exact               23

24   negative, and denote this average by F k ( x ). The innovation clipping map is odd. 24
25   Antithetic pairing preserves the marginal law and hence the expectation of the                 25

26   continuation.                                                                                  26

27     Draw an independent trajectory under the selected candidate and an independent               27

28   uniform date K. At that date write δ = aK − πK 1 and x a = x aK ( ZK ), xπ = xπK ( ZK ). 28

31     One observation is one antithetic pair, not its two dependent paths. Different                     31

32   query-pair cells use independent arrays. Equal pair counts at every query and                        32
     62

1    at every declared training stream identify their uniform finite average. For each              1

2    contrast, the report pools only its independent pair observations, clips each query            2

3    at its proved range, uses the maximum range in the independent nonidentical                    3

4    empirical-Bernstein bound, and averages numerical and tail allowances. Depen- 4
5    dence across methods is retained by forming the direct contrast before inference. 5
6    The primary family comprises 216 two-sided events and uses probability budget                  6

7    0.018. The separate scalar-accuracy family below uses 0.002. Their union remains               7

8    within the fixed total 0.02 budget.                                                            8

9                                                                                                   9

10                                                                                                  10

11     Arithmetic and Gaussian clipping. The numerical verifier uses the preserved pro- 11
12   tected polynomial for tanh, outward coefficient operations, and forward state-error            12

13   recursions with multiplier 1 + hβ. Fixed dyadic spectral proposals for the two                 13

14   declared coupling matrices are each certified by a fresh outward LDL calcula- 14
15   tion. Postdecision representatives are interval midpoints, so reconstructing their             15

16   error accounts does not depend on the last bits of a numerical singular-value                  16

17   or matrix-product routine. A deterministic state cap is computed from the fixed                17

18   query, bounded drift and reference actions, and the executed Gaussian cap. Produc- 18
19   tion error is bounded by β ψ times the state error plus its protected-function and             19

20   dot-product allowances. A terminal state error e at a cap M contributes at most                20

21   (γ/2)e(2M + e), before the separately charged scalar reductions. The source records            21

22   every component of this account.                                                               22

23     The finite economy retains untruncated Gaussian innovations. For cap z,                      23

24                                                                                                  24

                                     E{ G − clipz ( G )}2 ≤ 4ϕ(z)/z3 .

16   This is the integral of the derivative’s Lipschitz bound; it also covers one-sided         16

18   continuation before this bank. Binary64 construction of the affine actions receives its    18

19   own payoff allowance. All 128 scalar events use 65,536 independent antithetic pairs        19

20   each and the fixed probability allocation. The resulting reference covers every scalar     20

21   action in the interval, including unevaluated ones. Its scope is the specified finite      21

22   decision class; it is not a high-dimensional full-adapted-control or continuous-time       22

23   near-optimality assertion.                                                                 23

                             G.3. Scope of the scalar accuracy reference                        25

26     The protected quadratic upper bound controls the best action over the entire de- 26
27   clared scalar intervention segment, not merely a finite grid of evaluated actions. It is   27

28   therefore a distinct absolute accuracy object from the broad adapted-control bound. 28
29   It neither covers all high-dimensional current actions nor changes the original            29

30   continuous-time control problem into a finite scalar problem. The stored finite- 30
31   model coefficients, horizon, task law, scalar segment, and subsequent reference            31

32   policy are part of its definition.                                                         32
                                           NBO SUPPLEMENT                                             65

1      The interval law, numerical transfer, and whole-segment curvature are proved                        1

2    before their statistical use. A manufactured check of these identities verifies the                   2

3    calculation, not a successful economic outcome for a learned candidate. When                          3

4    a numerical scalar bound is reported, its event, selected scalar candidate, and                       4

5    comparison class must accompany it. No numerical value is inferred from the                           5

6    positivity of a curvature constant alone.                                                             6

7                                                                                                          7

8                       G.4. Decision value with binding task constraints                                  8

9      Temporary action constraints need not rule out a strict decision-value result. The                  9

10   interior assumption in Proposition 7 can be replaced by a stable-face condition. The                  10

11   relevant prediction norm then charges errors only in the directions in which each                     11

12   economic task can respond.                                                                            12

13     Retain the quadratic objectives and the common observation z = q + ε of Sec- 13
14   tion 6.1, with Eε = 0 and covariance Σ. For each task, fix a nonempty face Fj of its                  14

15   feasible polytope, a point a0j ∈ Fj , and a full-column-rank matrix L j spanning the                  15

16   parallel space of Fj . Put                                                                            16

17                                                                                                         17
                                                                     K
18                        R j = L j ( L⊤         −1 ⊤
                                       j Hj L j ) L j ,      WF = ∑        ω j h2j R j .                   18
                                                                    j =1
19                                                                                                         19

20   For a singleton face set R j = 0. Assume that WF is positive definite. Individual faces               20

21   may have binding constraints; the last condition requires their decision-relevant                     21

22   tangent spaces jointly to span the continuation coordinates.                                          22

23     Let D be a fixed scalar-gradient dictionary, independent of ε, and let PF project                   23

24   orthogonally onto the columns of WF1/2 D. Define                                                      24

25                                                                                                         25
           qbR = z,     qbC = WF−1/2 PF WF1/2 z,          y F = WF1/2 q,       Ω F = WF1/2 ΣWF1/2 .
26                                                                                                         26

27   The stable-face condition is that, for q, qbR , and qbC , the unique constrained maximizer            27

28   lies in the relative interior of the same deterministic face Fj , almost surely, for every            28

29   task. This is a condition on the original observation law; it does not condition an                   29

30   otherwise unrestricted noise distribution on a face-selection event. It can be verified               30

31   by the inactive slacks and the normal optimality inequalities on the prescribed noise                 31

32   support.                                                                                              32

1      The result gives a positive boundary-aware mechanism, not a universal ranking                  1

2    of methods. The faces and dictionary must satisfy the stated conditions for the full             2

3    observation law. If fitted actions switch faces, the displayed exact identity cannot be          3

4    obtained merely by selecting the observations with a favorable face. If WF is singular,          4

5    the present statement does not invoke an ordinary inverse; a lower-dimensional                   5

6    formulation requires a separately specified decision space. The nonlinear menu                   6

7    is evaluated by its own paired payoff intervals, rather than by asserting that its               7

8    fitted features or active sets satisfy this specialization. Identical projected estimators,      8

9    whether called Raw or NBO, still have identical decisions.                                       9

                                     H.3. Proof of Proposition S.17                                    19

20     Regularity and integrability. The drift is globally Lipschitz in the state, with con- 20
21   stant β, and bounded uniformly over admissible actions. Thus the physical equa- 21
22   tions have unique strong solutions with finite moments on the finite horizon. The                 22

23   actions are bounded and measurable at the left endpoint of their cell. The first three            23

24   derivatives of f are bounded. All stochastic integrals used below are consequently                24

25   square integrable, so their expectations vanish and Itô’s isometry applies without                25

26   an unaccounted localization limit. The quadratic terminal payoff and every product                26

27   below are integrable.                                                                             27

28                                                                                                     28

29     Derivative and generator bounds. The elementary identities for tanh give | tanh′ | ≤            29

30   1, | tanh′′ | ≤ L2 , and | tanh′′′ | ≤ 2. For a held action v, write                              30

27     Each original stored gain is a policy-minus-reference statistic. Under the verified        27

28   common-noise and initial-state identities, their exact-real reference terms coincide,        28

29   and cancel in the direct difference. Charging the complete stored-statistic error for        29

22   The implementation retains m = 0, . . . , 28. Writing z∗ = βT < 1, the successive ratios     22

23   in each remaining tail are bounded by z∗ /30, z∗ /31, and 3z∗ /32, respectively. If u        23

24   is the last retained term and r∗ < 1 is its tail-ratio bound, the omitted positive tail is   24

25   at most ur∗ /(1 − r∗ ). All sums, products, roots, differences of integral enclosures,       25

26   and the positive recursion (S.70) are rounded outward. The scalar Bk , Jk are their          26

27   original interval enclosures.                                                                27

28     For the implementation terms, the calculation takes the maximum, over the                  28

29   nine initial profiles, of the inherited forward-roundoff plus normal-clipping strong- 29
30   error bound. It also retains twice the complete original stored-statistic arithmetic         30

31   allowance and the original 10−12 allowance for the direct subtraction. The latter            31

32   includes no proposed cancellation of scalar quadrature or evaluation errors. The             32
                                          NBO SUPPLEMENT                                77

1    calculation is conditional on the same binary64 and ideal-iid-normal sampling            1

2    contract as the original verifier.                                                       2

3      This account applies when both compared outputs are held controllers. If both          3

4    outputs select the analytical schedule, their difference remains exactly zero. If        4

5    exactly one selects that schedule, the original complete schedule-relative transfer      5

6    is retained. A continuously varying analytical schedule is not silently treated as a     6

7    held controller.                                                                         7

8      Finally, let the original empirical-Bernstein event bound the mean of the same         8

9    clipped direct-difference observations, with its original clipping threshold and tail    9

10   allowance. Both the original deterministic transfer and (S.74) hold uniformly for the    10

11   two held controllers. Their minimum may therefore be used on that same event. This       11

12   operation spends no additional probability budget, changes no sample or stopping         12

13   rule, and makes no claim of a smaller empirical variance. The original endpoints         13

14   remain available together with the refined endpoints and the deterministic accounts      14

15   that distinguish them.                                                                   15

18                                                                                            18
       Condition on the complete executed policy ensemble, including all online check-
19                                                                                            19
     ing and stopping decisions. The final confirmation arrays are independent of that
20                                                                                            20
     information. For C > 0, set Zsj = (Ysj + C )/(2C ). These variables lie in [0, 1] and
21                                                                                            21
     are independent; their distributions may differ across streams. Apply Maurer and
22                                                                                            22
     Pontil (2009, Theorem 11) to the pooled vector and to its complement, allocating
23                                                                                            23
     δ/2 to each sign. After rescaling, the sampling radius is the first two terms in (29).
24                                                                                            24
     Equal path counts give
25                                                                                            25
                                                   1

26                                        EȲ =           EYs1 .                              26

27                                                     ∈S                                     27

28   The triangle inequality and the stream-specific transfer accounts bound the distance     28

29   between this expression and the desired finite-distribution mean by β̄. This proves      29

30   the interval claim. When C = 0, the clipped observations vanish identically and the      30

31   transfer account alone suffices. The conditional bound also holds unconditionally        31

32   by integration over the executed ensemble.                                               32
     78

1      At an online checkpoint, condition instead on the preceding execution history. 1
2    The policy at that checkpoint is fixed under this conditioning, and its new checking           2

3    bank is independent. The same argument applies to that registered checkpoint                   3

4    event. A union bound over the predeclared events covers the selected stopping                  4

5    time. Final method inference uses independent confirmation arrays, so it does not              5

6    treat the array that triggered a favorable stop as an ordinary fixed-policy sample. 6
7    Summing the four family allocations establishes the stated 0.05 error bound. On the            7

8    simultaneous stream event, (30) follows by counting the streams whose lower or                 8

9    upper endpoints cross the target.                                                    Q.E.D.    9

15                                                                                                             15
     I.1.1. Executed calibration and interpretation
16                                                                                                             16

17     The replication program trains a homothetic neural subfamily: a positive value                          17

18   coefficient, a sigmoid consumption-share output, and a projected portfolio-share                          18

19   output. These are neural output parameters in a known invariant representation, not                       19

20   an unrestricted multilayer approximation. The two Adam optimizers have learning                           20

21   rates 0.025 and 0.008; each outer iteration contains three critic updates and one                         21

22   actor update. There are 1,600 outer iterations and seeds 0, 1, 2. All attempted runs,                     22

23   including their coefficient and policy traces, are saved. These calibrations optimize                     23

24   the stationary coefficient equation and then check the resulting coefficient against                      24

25   the matching bequest; the coefficient error is a boundary error in that finite-horizon                    25

26   interpretation, not a boundary condition hard-imposed during these particular                             26

27   updates.                                                                                                  27

28     Table I.I reports quantities generated by the new code. The normalized policy- 28
29   evaluation residual is below 4 × 10−16 in these runs. These results establish that                        29

30   the revised graph and formulas agree in the exactly representable laboratory. They                        30

31   do not establish a broad advantage over a direct analytical calculation. In the exit                      31

32   laboratory, grids with 40, 80, 160, and 320 intervals recover the grid value to within                    32
     80

1    3 × 10−15 ; the wrong smooth sequence has scaled residual between 1.984 and 1.999. 1
2    All grid nodes, not a selected interior sample, enter that diagnostic.                                   2

28                                                                                                            28
     I.2.1. A well-defined validation problem at the retained parameters
29                                                                                                            29

30     The numerical calibration uses (S.85) as a finite-horizon matching-bequest test,                       30

31   not as an unqualified infinite-horizon theorem. Choose T > 0, stop wealth on leav- 31
32   ing [ x, x̄ ] with 0 < x < x̄ < ∞, and impose the candidate Kx b /b on the stopping                      32
                                        NBO SUPPLEMENT                                      81

1    boundary and at T. Consumption shares are in a compact positive interval contain- 1
2    ing 0.0455, and portfolio shares are in a compact interval containing 0.3.                  2

3      For these retained parameters, a > 0 and b < 0. Since c ranges over a compact             3

4    positive interval, there exist 0 < Y < Y < ∞ such that f (c, Y/b) ≤ 0 and f (c, Y/b) ≥      4

5    0 for every feasible c. Indeed, c a Y −a/b tends to zero as Y ↓ 0 and to infinity as Y ↑ ∞. 5
6    Enlarge the interval so that it contains the boundary candidate. The constants Y/b          6

7    and Y/b are respectively lower and upper barriers for the backward utility equation. 7
8    Scalar BSDE comparison therefore keeps every policy utility in this interval. The           8

9    aggregator is Lipschitz there. The smooth homothetic candidate maximizes the                9

10   Hamiltonian and matches all stopping payoffs; Theorem S.1 identifies it as the value        10

11   of this specified finite-horizon problem.                                                   11

12     The choice of a matching bequest is an analytical validation device. It does              12

13   not establish that the same candidate is selected by every infinite-horizon recur- 13
14   sive formulation. A separate infinite-horizon application must check its utility- 14
15   process class and transversality, particularly when risk aversion and intertemporal- 15
16   complementarity parameters straddle one (Herdegen et al., 2021). The paper retains          16

17   that economic application, but does not use a scalar root to bypass its selection           17

18   issue.                                                                                      18

19     A critic can enforce bV > 0 through bvξ = exp( Nξ ). With a positive terminal             19

20   target, logarithmic interpolation between bG and the neural output enforces both            20

21   the terminal condition and the power domain. Additional barriers or bounded                 21

22   transformations enforce a declared invariant interval. Such transformations are             22

23   stated in the architecture, not imposed retrospectively after a failed evaluation.          23

24                                                                                               24

25                                                                                               25
     I.2.2. Stability and baseline design
26                                                                                               26

27     The recursive experiment in Table I.I tests the corrected sign, coefficient, and          27

28   block graph. It does not compare independent DDPG training trajectories. The                28

29   reviewed manuscript’s constructed oscillating curve and manually entered coordi- 29
30   nates remain in the archival source, but have no evidentiary role in this paper. A          30

31   matched neural comparison must use the same utility process, domain, bequest,               31

32   action restrictions, training budget, and held-out evaluation quantities. It must           32
     82

1    report all failed seeds and value-domain violations. Neither a zero-centered target                1

2    nor three successful homothetic calibrations establishes general relative stability.               2

3                                                                                                       3

4                           I.3. Endogenous Preferences and Portfolio Choice                            4

5      The endogenous-preference application retains its internal adjustment margin. 5
6    Let u be risk aversion, X wealth, and θ adjustment effort. On a declared interval                  6

7    [u, ū] ⊂ (1, ∞), specify the reflected process                                                    7

            du = θ dt + σu dW u + dLu − dLū ,                                                 (S.86)

11                                                                                                      11
     The nondecreasing local-time regulators act only at their respective boundaries.
12                                                                                                      12
     This supplies the state mechanism that a bounded adjustment actor does not supply.
13                                                                                                      13
     The numerical reference uses [1.2, 3], so the utility singularity at u = 1 is never
14                                                                                                      14
     reached. In an alternative positive-state model one could specify a multiplicative
15                                                                                                      15
     or transformed diffusion, but that would be a different primitive and must be
16                                                                                                      16
     identified as such.

     I.3.3. Independent two-state reference and identification                                          12

13     The executable reference solves the finite-horizon problem with T = 1, u ∈ [1.2, 3],             13

14   wealth stopped at [0.5, 2], and G (u, X ) = X 1−u /(1 − u) at exit and terminal time. Pa- 14
15   rameters are (r, µ, σX , σu , ϱ, ρ) = (0.02, 0.08, 0.2, 0.08, −0.3, 0.04). The finite action set   15

16   is the product of c/X ∈ {0.02, 0.05, 0.1}, p ∈ {0, 0.375, 0.75}, and θ ∈ {−0.15, 0, 0.15}. 16
17   A four-point Brownian cubature with positive interpolation weights performs back- 17
18   ward induction. The wealth boundary is imposed at every time step; preference                      18

19   overshoots are reflected. This reference defines a finite-action numerical approxima- 19
20   tion, not continuous-action optimality.                                                            20

21     At (u, X ) = (2.1, 1), the original 17 × 25 state grid and 20 time steps give values             21

22   −11.915211 and −11.956983 for k = 1 and k = 5. The 25 × 37 grid and 40 time                        22

23   steps give −11.866768 and −11.908738. Both select (c/X, p, θ ) = (0.1, 0.75, −0.15)                23

24   at that point. The approximately 0.048 refinement change is reported separately                    24

25   from training error. The higher-cost value is weakly lower at every recorded node                  25

26   and time. These original references remain useful consistency checks; Section I.3.4                26

27   supplies actual neural fits and shows how strongly the finite action domain affects                27

28   their economic comparison.                                                                         28

29     The model also specifies what a structural estimation exercise would require. For                29

30   observations Yj = M(St j , at j ; ϑ ) + ε j , let m(ϑ ) denote selected population moments         30

31   of the induced observation process. Local identification requires a rank condition                 31

32   on Dϑ m, after accounting for latent preference states, normalization parameters,                  32
                                          NBO SUPPLEMENT                                  85

1    and observation noise. Variation of a simulated policy with k alone is insufficient. 1
2    Numerical value and policy errors must additionally be propagated into moment              2

3    errors. The endogenous-preference application is therefore retained as an economic         3

4    model with an explicit internal margin, not used as evidence that its structural           4

5    parameters have already been identified from data.                                         5

6      The next comparison is the retained continuous-action study evidence under               6

7    its original enclosure. The new signed envelope and fresh nested-grid policies are         7

8    reported separately in Section I.8; historical failure counts are not overwritten.         8

9                                                                                               9
     I.3.4. Neural preference policies and the role of the action oracle
10                                                                                              10

11     We compute the original preference-adjustment economy with the cardinal utility,         11

12   shock correlation, reflected preference state, and wealth stopping payoff specified        12

13   above. The main grid has 17 preference points, 25 log-wealth points, and 20 time           13

14   steps over a unit horizon. Adjustment cost is k = 1. The expanded control box is           14

15                                                                                              15
                  m = c/X ∈ [0.02, 0.30],        p ∈ [0, 1.5],    θ ∈ [−0.45, 0.45].
16                                                                                              16

17   The finite-action comparison uses the 175 triples formed by five consumption shares,       17

18   five portfolio shares, and seven adjustment efforts, including all endpoints. This is      18

19   the same constrained economy across the matched finite methods, not a change of            19

20   utility normalization.                                                                     20

21     Two experiments distinguish action representation from action optimization. 21
22   First, the finite-policy study finite study trains two-hidden-layer, width-32 tanh         22

23   critics and categorical actors. At each backward step it computes the complete 175- 23
24   action payoff vector using the current continuation critic. Its exact argmax provides      24

25   a label, and the vector also supplies the actor’s expected action-loss objective. Thus     25

26   the training oracle is exhaustive, even when the final actor is accurate. It is a useful   26

27   neural policy-representation experiment, but not evidence that the actor avoided           27

28   Bellman maximization.                                                                      28

29     Second, continuous-action study trains a continuous-output actor directly                29

30   through its own positive-weight payoff. It never receives an all-action value vector       30

31   or an exact argmax label. The actor receives 160 Adam updates per time level; the          31

32   continuation critic receives 320 Adam updates and up to 300 L-BFGS iterations, with        32
     86

1    failed polishing steps rolled back. The actor-free direct comparator optimizes eight                       1

2    independent bounded controls at every state for the same 160 iterations, followed                          2

3    by the same critic fit. Every restart and rejected action query is charged. Neither                        3

4    procedure invokes a Riccati solution, an analytically invariant policy class, or the                       4

5    independently computed reference during training.                                                          5

6                                                                                                               6

7      Finite-action neural policies. The raw finite-policy study actor is the primary                          7

8    neural representation result. Its full dynamic policy loss is below the declared 0.1                       8

9    target for each fixed seed. A top-four shortlist and a separately reported correction                      9

10   map further reduce the loss. The correction map is not part of the raw network. 10
11   The complete finite policy, not only one-step deviations against a fitted critic, is                       11

12   evaluated by independent backward recursion.                                                               12

13                                                                                                              13

1      Alternative representations and economic units. Additional same-model compar- 1
2    isons use a gated neural continuation architecture and tensor Chebyshev projection. 2
3    The gated method uses DGM-style state-dependent gates (Sirignano and Spiliopou- 3
4    los, 2018), but here it is fitted to finite Bellman targets, not called a mesh-free         4

5    differential DGM solver. The projection exploits the same two-state geometry and            5

6    imposes the same wealth-boundary lifting. Degrees, parameter counts, all-action             6

7    costs, failures, and actual accuracy appear in the supplement. These are executed           7

8    post-review comparisons, not results attributed to the earlier unexecuted develop- 8
9    ment protocol. A generic semilinear BSDE comparator is not substituted for this             9

10   controlled-covariance, stopped problem without reformulating its second-order               10

11   control term.                                                                               11

12     All losses are in the specified cardinal utility units. The optimal finite-model value    12

13   at the center is approximately −4.8410, and the initial value range is approximately        13

14   [−9.1599, −0.1250]. A loss of 0.1 is about 2.066 percent of the center’s absolute utility   14

15   magnitude; this scaling is not a consumption-equivalent welfare percentage. Endoge- 15
16   nous curvature and adjustment costs make a common consumption-equivalent                    16

17   conversion a separate economic calculation.                                                 17

21     Action bounds and controlled refinements. The sensitivity study retains both              21

22   adjustment-cost values, the original 27-action truncation, 175 and 1,053 actions            22

23   in the expanded box, a wider action box, wider wealth bounds, and finer space– 23
24   time grids. Consumption and portfolio upper bounds remain frequently active. 24
25   Expanding the box changes the constrained economy; refining actions inside one              25

26   box does not. continuous-action study additionally evaluates the same frozen contin- 26
27   uous policy, bilinearly interpolated in state and held on its original time schedule,       27

28   on three nested state–time levels. This isolates changes in policy evaluation from          28

29   retraining. The supplemental differences are observed refinement diagnostics, not           29

30   an estimated order promoted to a continuous-time error bound. The original contin- 30
31   uous control problem, its utility normalization, and its comparative-static questions       31

32   remain the economic objects of interest.                                                    32

19   The deviations change both current flow utility and the transition of the contin- 19

23     A temporal-self NBO uses two policy-evaluation critics for (S.98) and one actor                        23

24   for (S.99). The current actor does not optimize the sum of future selves’ parameter                      24

25   objectives. A target actor can stabilize a numerical iteration, but its agreement                        25

26   with the current actor is not an equilibrium certificate. The relevant diagnostics are                   26

     The expression on the right is strictly increasing in m on its admissible interval, starts
5                                                                                                            5

6                                                                                                            6
     homothetic class. Its derivative with respect to β is negative because 1/λ1 > 1/λ2 .
7                                                                                                            7

8                                                                                                            8

10                                                                                                           10

11                                                                                                           11

12                                                                                                           12

13                                                                                                           13

1      Given rivals’ Markov policies a−i , a player’s best-response equation maximizes               1

6    A Markov-perfect equilibrium is a feasible profile satisfying these simultaneous                6

9    therefore does not identify this equilibrium.                                                   9

     I.5.1. Player-specific differentiation and deviation bounds
11                                                                                                   11

12     Player i’s critic is trained with every policy frozen. Player i’s actor then differenti- 12
13   ates only                                                                                       13

14                               a (ϕi ),sg( a−i )                                                   14
                          −Eν H i i                  [sg(Vi ), sg( DVi ), sg( D2 Vi )].    (S.102)
15                                                                                                   15

16   The updated parameter is ϕi , not the full vector of all players’ parameters. Rival             16

17   actions are detached, and player j’s loss never contributes to player i’s actor gradient. 17
18   An explicit regression test exercises this graph.                                               18

19     For each player, define the unilateral Hamiltonian gap by maximizing Hi over                  19

20   ai while keeping rivals fixed. Applying Theorem S.1 to that best-response prob- 20
21   lem bounds its finite-horizon exploitability by 2e Li T Bi + A Li ( T )(2ei + ηi ). In a dis- 21
22   counted finite-state game, Theorem S.2 gives (2δi + ϵi )/(1 − qi ). These are player- 22
23   specific bounds; a sum of critic losses is not a substitute. They remain valid without          23

24   uniqueness or convergence of the simultaneous policy-update dynamics, provided                  24

25   the reported profile satisfies the stated errors and best-response comparison hy- 25
26   potheses.                                                                                       26

27     The static Cournot diagnostic makes the distinction visible. With payoffs qi (1 −             27

28   qi − q j ), symmetric Nash output is 1/3 per firm. Joint-profit maximization gives              28

29   a symmetric allocation 1/4. At the latter profile, a firm optimally deviates to 3/8,            29

30   earning a gain of 1/64. A joint objective can therefore be perfectly optimized and              30

31   still fail a unilateral equilibrium test. This arithmetic is included as a mandatory            31

32   regression check.                                                                               32
                                         NBO SUPPLEMENT                                   93

1    I.5.2. Symmetry and market scaling                                                         1

2                                                                                               2
       Identical initial weights do not prove equilibrium uniqueness or permutation
3                                                                                               3
     equivariance. An equivariant actor can be specified using own capital and a
4                                                                                               4
     permutation-invariant aggregation of rival states. Even then, one must check unilat-
5                                                                                               5
     eral deviations rather than infer equilibrium from architectural symmetry. Multiple
6                                                                                               6
     starting profiles and unsuccessful best-response searches belong in a numerical
7                                                                                               7
     report.
8                                                                                               8
       Under fixed market size, positive constant capital per firm cannot be called
9                                                                                               9
     the competitive limit while total output tends to infinity and the truncated price
10                                                                                              10
     tends to zero. Maintaining that capital entails investment and adjustment costs.
11                                                                                              11
     Under M N = N, positive capital per firm may instead be compatible with a growing
12                                                                                              12
     market, but it describes a different sequence of economies. The original unsupported
13                                                                                              13
     capital table remains in the archive. Section I.5.3 replaces its evidentiary role with a
14                                                                                              14
     fully specified finite dynamic game and independent best-response calculations,
15                                                                                              15
     without asserting convergence of unrestricted neural game training.
16                                                                                              16

17                                                                                              17
     I.5.3. Neural dynamic capital policies and full unilateral deviations
18                                                                                              18

19     For two firms, we retain the capital game with A = 1, P0 = 2, δ = 0.1, σK = 0.15,        19

20   ρ = 0.04, T = 3, investment in [0, 0.8], and capital in [0.05, 1.5]2 . Terminal or joint- 20
21   domain exit pays 0.5Ki . Baseline adjustment coefficients are b1 = b2 = 1; the earlier     21

22   asymmetric experiment has b2 = 1.2. The principal neural grid has 17 × 17 states,          22

23   30 time steps, and 11 actions per player. Positive cubature and interpolation define       23

24   the finite game. Market normalizations M2 = 1 and M2 = 2 are distinct economies,           24

25   not evidence for an uncomputed large-population limit.                                     25

26     At each time and state, the finite-policy study calculation trains separate critics      26

27   and actors. It constructs both full 11 × 11 continuation-payoff matrices. The training     27

28   labels are a pure one-step equilibrium when one exists and a minimum-maximum- 28
29   defect pair otherwise. The actor’s own objective is differentiated with the rival          29

30   action and continuation critic fixed. Thus this study actually trains neural strategic     30

31   policies, but it also pays for an exhaustive joint-action training oracle. Raw policies,   31

32   three-by-three shortlist policies, and any corrected policies are distinct stored arrays. 32
     94

1      After freezing the entire profile, an independent backward optimization permits                       1

2    every own action at every state and time against the fixed rival policy. Its value                      2

3    minus the profile payoff is the unilateral exploitability map. This is not a one- 3
4    period test with the approximate continuation held fixed. The raw and final profiles                    4

5    meet the 0.1 absolute-profit target in every principal finite-policy study case; the                    5

6    full-oracle guard is unused in those cases.                                                             6

20     Five state-time nodes across the six neural runs lack a pure one-step equilibrium                     20

21   for the fitted continuation matrices. Their locations and defects are reported in                       21

22   the supplement. A minimum-defect selection at those nodes is not called an exact                        22

23   equilibrium. Nevertheless, the complete dynamic best-response bound remains                             23

24   valid for the resulting profile: its proof does not assume that every fitted one-step                   24

25   game has a pure solution. Moreover, for fixed Markov rivals, randomized or history- 25
26   dependent own deviations cannot improve on the finite Markov best-response                              26

27   value, because each conditional action mixture is bounded by its largest pure-action                    27

28   payoff. The calculation therefore checks such unilateral deviations as well; it does                    28

29   not compute a mixed-strategy equilibrium profile or a continuous-action game.                           29

30     continuous-action study adds actor-free neural continuation fitting and classical                     30

31   backward selection for both market normalizations. The neural comparator allocates                      31

32   480 critic Adam updates per time level rather than finite-policy study’s 320 critic                     32
                                            NBO SUPPLEMENT                                            95

1    plus 160 actor updates, with the same L-BFGS cap. Counts and timings reveal the                       1

2    difference between nominal update matching and matched wall time. Both methods                        2

3    receive their own independent full dynamic best responses. The earlier six classical                  3

4    game cases, asymmetric costs, alternative tie rules, boundary frequencies, coarse                     4

5    counterexample, and finer-grid results remain in the supplement and raw record.                       5

                                I.6. Scalability and the Diffusion Trace
29                                                                                                         29

30     The original multi-stock consumption-savings specification has additive utility,                    30

31   independent transitions, and no common resource constraint. It decomposes as                          31

32   V (k ) = ∑ j v j (k j ). A separability-aware comparator solves the one-dimensional com- 32
     96

1    ponents, and identical components can be reused. An unreduced sparse grid is not            1

2    an appropriate sole comparator for that economy.                                            2

3      To test a coupled generator, the new laboratory instead uses                              3

4                                                   Z ∞                                          4
            dX = ( AX + a)dt + ΣdW,         sup E         e−ρt [− X ⊤ QX − a⊤ a]dt.   (S.103)
5                                            a      0                                            5

6                                                            √                                   6
     The drift is a nonnormal dense matrix A = −0.7I + 0.06Z/ d, where Z is generated
7                                                                                                7
     by the fixed model seed 991 + d. The payoff matrix is Q = diag(1, . . . , 2) + 0.311⊤ /d.
8                                                                                                8
     The code verifies that AQ ̸= QA, so the common independent-coordinate reduction
9                                                                                                9
     of the original benchmark is unavailable. All methods still exploit the declared
10                                                                                               10
     quadratic structure. A Riccati solver is therefore the appropriate strong reference,
11                                                                                               11
     rather than a deliberately unreduced general grid.
12                                                                                               12
       For V = − x ⊤ Px − c0 and a = −Kx, policy evaluation solves
13                                                                                               13

14        ( A − K − ρI/2)⊤ P + P( A − K − ρI/2) = −( Q + K ⊤ K ),        c0 = tr(ΣΣ⊤ P)/ρ.       14

15                                                                                    (S.104)    15

16   Hamiltonian improvement gives K = P. A positive semidefinite P is representable             16

17   by a signed sum of squared linear neural units; the actor is linear. The exact block        17

18   laboratory uses a Lyapunov solve for this critic and exact actor improvement. It is         18

19   an exactly representable NBO subfamily and a matrix-identity test, not evidence             19

20   that generic stochastic neural optimization has the same convergence rate.                  20

21     Across dimensions 2, 5, 10, 20, 50 and three stabilizing actor initializations, the       21

22   block iteration takes six steps. The maximum error in P, in operator norm, is below         22

23   10−13 relative to an independent algebraic Riccati solver. The policy-evaluation            23

24   coefficient residual is below 10−12 . On the unit Euclidean ball, the actor gap is          24

25   bounded by ∥K − P∥22 , and the residual bound follows from the operator norm of             25

26   the quadratic coefficient error. The program saves matrices, iteration traces, and          26

27   CPU wall times for both methods. These very short, single-process timings are               27

28   diagnostics and do not establish a fixed-accuracy runtime ranking.                          28

21     The old twenty-dimensional fitting box is exited by essentially all recorded paths. 21
22   Wider-domain fitting eliminates observed exits in the recorded audits and changes             22

23   both payoff agreement and method differences. Zero observed exits in 512 paths                23

24   is not a zero exit-probability theorem. Likewise, these intervals quantify point- 24
25   wise Monte Carlo uncertainty under a specified Euler scheme. They are neither                 25

26   simultaneous confidence statements across all 36 comparisons nor bounds on time               26

27   bias, optimal value, or the global differential residual. The experiment identifies a         27

28   consequential coverage failure and its measured correction without redefining the             28

29   unbounded economic state space as a stopped box.                                              29

30     The one-state consumption certificate has a different evidentiary status. finite- 30
31   policy study independently replays its previously generated complete leaf cover               31

32   with mpmath.iv at 40 decimal digits, without importing the original interval                  32
     100

1    arithmetic implementation. For the stored seed-11 networks, the NBO policy-loss          1

2    bound is about 0.04961 and the direct-HJB bound about 0.04967. Both meet the             2

3    stated threshold. This is an independent interval-arithmetic replay on the previously    3

4    generated complete cover, not independent network training, independent cover            4

5    generation, or a formal machine proof. Together with the new complete three- 5
6    control cover on the two-state preference grid, it demonstrates two different ways       6

7    of operationalizing verification. Neither removes the dimensional cost of covering       7

8    an unrestricted high-dimensional continuous state space.                                 8

     I.6.3. Exact and stochastic trace computation
10                                                                                            10

11     Let Σ include any covariance factor and set B = Σ⊤ D2 vΣ. For independent probes       11

12   with identity covariance,                                                                12

13
                                                    1 K ⊤                                     13
                                 q = tr( B),    qb = ∑ zk Bzk .                     (S.106)
14                                                  K k =1                                    14

15                                                                                            15

16
     Gaussian probes have variance 2∥ B∥2F /K. If r = d − q/2, then                           16

17
                                  E(d − qb/2)2 = r2 + 41 Var(qb).                   (S.107)   17

18                                                                                            18

19   The extra term depends on the critic. Unbiased trace estimation therefore does not       19

20   give an unbiased squared-residual objective.                                             20

21     Two conditionally independent probe banks repair this particular bias:                 21

22                                                                                            22
                     L = (d − qb(1) /2)(d − qb(2) /2),
                     b                                    E[ b
                                                             L | t, x, ξ ] = r2 .   (S.108)
23                                                                                            23

24   With dominated differentiation, its gradient is also unbiased. The sample product        24

25   need not be nonnegative and can have substantial variance, so this is an objective       25

26   identity rather than a guarantee of stable optimization. Exact low-dimensional           26

27   derivatives remain the independent check. If the actor or deterministic term depends     27

28   on the same probes, conditional independence must be re-established; the simple          28

29   identity cannot be reused automatically.                                                 29

30     The relevant computational cost depends on the network evaluation and differ- 30
31   entiation cost, Brownian rank, covariance factorization, sample count, optimization      31

32   iterations, and requested accuracy. Forming a dense Hessian involves quadratic           32
                                        NBO SUPPLEMENT                                    101

1    storage; Hessian-vector products can avoid it. Neither operation count by itself is a        1

2    total-complexity theorem. Active control bounds do not universally create gradient           2

3    discontinuities, and a noisy trace cannot make a correctly projected box action leave        3

4    its box. Feasibility and value-derivative accuracy are separate diagnostics.                 4

5      The independent-bank estimator is also exercised during actual training in the             5

6    coupled ten-state economy. One implementation uses two independent banks of                  6

7    two Gaussian Hessian-vector probes; the matched single-bank ablation squares a               7

8    four-probe estimate. Exact traces are used only for the common held-out diagnostics. 8
9    All three seeds, objective signs, optimization histories, and policy-payoff samples          9

10   are saved. The two training procedures perform similarly at the retained covariance          10

11   scale; unbiasedness alone does not establish a finite-budget advantage. A separate           11

12   Monte Carlo test checks both the product objective and its parameter gradient                12

13   against exact trace expressions with declared tolerances. These calculations validate        13

14   the estimator’s execution without identifying a random training objective with a             14

15   deterministic certificate.                                                                   15

16                                                                                                16

17                                                                                                17
                       I.7. Validated Evaluation and Action Improvement
18                                                                                                18
       The neural Bellman map must account for two distinct approximation decisions:
19                                                                                                19
     which continuation values are passed to the next stage, and how tightly the admis-
20                                                                                                20
     sible action supremum is bounded. This section supplies an explicit output rule
21                                                                                                21
     for the first decision and a signed enclosure for the second. Neither construction
22                                                                                                22
     presumes that a stationary point of the training loss is economically optimal. Both
23                                                                                                23
     apply to the original preference economy and leave its controls, state law, and
24                                                                                                24
     stopping convention unchanged.
25                                                                                                25

26                                                                                                26
     I.7.1. An evaluation rule with a prescribed nodal error

18                                                                                                     18

19                                                                                                     19

20                                                                                                     20

21     The guard gives an implementable meaning to the evaluation component of a                       21

22   vanishing-perturbation condition. It does not supply the action-improvement error,                22

23   the consistency error of a stopped diffusion approximation, or convergence of an un- 23
24   corrected network away from the nodes. Its cost can be substantial: in the numerical              24

25   economy it corrects a large fraction of fitted nodal values. We consequently count                25

26   the correction memory and compare against an actor-free method with the identical                 26

27   guard. The construction is not evidence of neural compression when almost every                   27

28   node is retained.                                                                                 28

29                                                                                                     29
     I.7.2. Signed enclosures for continuous actions
30                                                                                                     30

31     Fix a state-time node and a continuation vector w. Write Qw ( a) for its one-step               31

32   payoff. The action set is the full compact box of the preference economy. Let B = [ℓ, r ]         32
                                                 NBO SUPPLEMENT                                              103

1    be an action rectangle with centre c. A natural interval calculation supplies an upper                        1

2    bound U nat ( B), while independent feasible evaluations supply a lower incumbent                             2

3    L.                                                                                                            3

4         The continuation is bilinear within state cells and continuous across their internal                     4

5    faces. Reflection changes its one-sided slopes. A stopped transition is different:                            5

6    the implementation switches between interpolated continuation and the specified                               6

7    stopping payoff, which need not agree away from boundary grid points. We call B                               7

8    regular when every cubature transition stays entirely inside, or entirely outside, each                       8

9    wealth stopping face. Only on such a box do we use derivative information. All                                9

10   intersected interpolation cells and both sides of reflection folds enter the derivative                       10

11   hull.                                                                                                         11

12        Suppose Gj ( B) = [ g−     +
                               j , g j ] encloses every one-sided partial derivative with respect                  12

13   to action component j on a regular box. The mean-value upper enclosure is                                     13

14                                                                                                                 14

15
           U mv ( B) = Qw (c) + ∑ max{ g−                 −                 +                 +
                                        j (ℓ j − c j ), g j (r j − c j ), g j (ℓ j − c j ), g j (r j − c j )},

     where Qw (c) is an outward point upper bound. The algorithm uses the smaller of                               18

     this bound and U nat ( B). If g−                                                    +
