24        T HEOREM S.19—Signed action cover: Assume that the natural enclosures contain                            24

25   their respective payoffs, that the derivative hulls contain all one-sided derivatives on each                 25

26   regular box, and that feasible point values and arithmetic bounds are outward. Start from a                   26

27   cover of the action box and apply subdivision, reduction to a verified maximizing face, and                   27

28   removal of a box only when its upper bound is at most L + ε. At any interruption, retain                      28

29   the upper bound of every unresolved box. Then                                                                 29

30                                                                                                                 30

31                                                                                                                 31
                                sup Qw ( a) ≤ max{ L + ε,            max        U ( B)}.                 (S.111)
32                              a∈A                              B unresolved                                      32
     104

1    The bound remains valid when a depth, work, or time budget is exhausted. If r action coordi- 1
2    nates have verified strict derivative signs throughout a regular parent box, maximization   2

3    over that box reduces to a face of dimension at most d a − r.                               3

4                                                                                                4
       The last statement describes a source of computational improvement, not a
5                                                                                                5
     dimension-free worst-case bound. Under a K-Lipschitz gradient, a regular stationary
6                                                                                                6
     box of diameter s has mean-value excess of order Ks2 when its point evaluation
7                                                                                                7
     error is negligible. Boxes that cross stopping switches, have ambiguous derivative
8                                                                                                8
     signs, or contain many interpolation cells can remain expensive. Our experiment
9                                                                                                9
     measures their actual cost and keeps unresolved bounds; it does not assume that
10                                                                                               10
     every box has favorable structure.
11                                                                                               11
       The operational NBO output is now explicit: the frozen policy representation,
12                                                                                               12
     its guarded continuation vector when used, an independently propagated policy
13                                                                                               13
     interval, and a complete-action optimal upper envelope. A nodal lookup policy is
14                                                                                               14
     the object certified in the preference experiment. An off-grid interpolation of that
15                                                                                               15
     policy is a different deployment rule and is evaluated separately. No online search
16                                                                                               16
     is charged to a stored lookup table; constructing that table includes every actor,
17                                                                                               17
     critic, and local-search query.
18                                                                                               18

19                                                                                               19
                           I.8. Cost, Refinement, and Economic Accuracy
20                                                                                               20

21     The preceding applications define the original economic scope of NBO. We now              21

22   evaluate the guarded map and signed cover on the same preference economy, and               22

23   connect the high-dimensional experiment to an absolute economic benchmark. The              23

24   comparison retains every earlier failure. In particular, a new upper envelope does          24

25   not turn an actor-plus-search policy into an actor-only policy, and retraining on a         25

26   finer grid does not repair the off-grid performance of a previously frozen policy.          26

27                                                                                               27
     I.8.1. A tighter certificate for identical frozen policies
28                                                                                               28

29     The reference specification has 17 × 25 state nodes, twenty dates, and the continu- 29
30   ous action box [0.02, 0.30] × [0, 1.5] × [−0.45, 0.45]. The ratios constrain consumption,   30

31   leverage, and preference adjustment in the declared economy. They are economic              31

32   primitives, not bounds on an unconstrained optimum. Active consumption or port- 32
                                              NBO SUPPLEMENT                                              105

1    folio limits remain meaningful features of this constrained specification; changing                        1

2    them changes the economy.                                                                                  2

3      We freeze the nine previously computed actor-plus-search, direct-plus-search,                            3

4    and search-only policies, including their original seeds. The complete-action upper                        4

5    envelope is recomputed independently of these policies’ lower payoff envelopes. 5
6    The new cover uses a one-step tolerance of 0.0002 and retains every unresolved                             6

7    upper bound. Its output can be shared across policies only because their action set,                       7

8    transition rule, domain, and payoff normalization coincide.                                                8

     I.8.2. The whole-domain refinement failure and its repair                                            23

24     A frozen policy has to be distinguished from a policy refitted on a new grid. The                  24

25   complete historical refinement arrays reveal large losses away from the centre. Their                25

26   maximum positive reference advantages, locations, quantiles, boundary-region                         26

27   summaries, and action saturation rates are retained in the complete numerical                        27

28   archive indexed in Appendix S. The largest historical discrepancy is approximately                   28

29   2.1234 at high risk aversion and wealth close to the lower stopping boundary. Centre                 29

30   values alone do not summarize that experiment. These discrepancies are feasible- 30
31   reference lower bounds on policy regret, not upper bounds for the continuous                         31

32   economy.                                                                                             32

21     A new complete-action envelope is computed on the fine grid, not interpolated                      21

22   from the coarse certificate. It is applied to all twelve fine-grid policies, including               22

23   the six unguarded runs. This separates three facts: the failure of a frozen coarse- 23
24   grid policy; the accuracy achieved by retraining within a declared nodal economy;                    24

25   and the additional error required to transfer a result to a continuously monitored                   25

26   diffusion. The first remains in the record, the second is measured, and the third is not             26

27   assigned a zero value. Two levels alone do not establish an asymptotic convergence                   27

28   rate.                                                                                                28

29     The deployment specification is a stored state-time action table with its saved                    29

30   neural proposal and continuation correction. Evaluating this table at its nodes                      30

31   requires no online maximizing search. Interpolating the table at off-grid states                     31

32   invokes a different policy rule; the historical experiment shows why this distinction                32
     108

1    matters. A raw actor without a table or local improvement has a different memory         1

2    requirement and is not represented by the hybrid loss bounds.                            2

     I.8.3. A model-specific welfare account
5                                                                                             5

6      An absolute utility gap is not a consumption-equivalent percentage when the            6

7    utility exponent is itself a controlled state. We therefore compute a defined com- 7
8    pensating transfer. Hold the saved policy and its state law fixed, leave preference- 8
9    adjustment costs and the stopping bequest unchanged, and add externally financed         9

10   consumption so that flow consumption becomes (1 + e)ct Xt . This intervention            10

11   changes felicity but does not charge its financing to wealth. It is a compensation       11

12   experiment, not another admissible self-financed policy in the original box.             12

13     Let Jeπ be the compensated payoff and U the common optimal upper envelope. 13
14   A verified lower envelope J π
                                 e
                                   ≥ U at every active starting node and date implies 14
15   that this transfer compensates the policy’s loss everywhere in the declared nodal        15

16   economy. We search for a common e using monotonicity of CRRA felicity in con- 16
17   sumption and verify the final inequality by outward propagation. Table I.XII gives       17

18   the resulting upper percentages. These conservative transfers can be economically        18

19   substantial even when the absolute utility bound is below 0.1; the threshold is not      19

20   described as a universally small welfare loss.                                           20

21                                                                                            21

22                                                                                            22
     I.8.4. A global benchmark for the coupled capital economy
23                                                                                            23

24     A comparison of two frozen policies does not indicate how far either is from           24

25   the economic optimum. We supplement the paired simulations by a bound on the             25

26   original, continuous-time, dense nonlinear capital economy. It does not rely on          26

27   a Riccati equation, separability of the optimal policy, a state mesh, or a sampled       27

28   residual.                                                                                28

31   is Y − χ(Y − Y )2 , with χ = 0.03. The drift of Yi is a + κ tanh(( BY )i ) − mi − (σI2 + 31
32   σC2 )/2. Common noise affects the mean but cancels from cross-sectional dispersion. 32

     I.9.1. Merton consumption and transversality                                                               30

31     Write b = 1 − γ and V = KX b /b. Then VX = KX −γ and VXX = −γKX −γ−1 . Sub- 31

                              bK b

5                                                                                                  5

6                                                                                                  6

7                                                                                                  7

     I.9.2. Recursive coefficient and the utility interval
17                                                                                                 17
       For f (c, V ) = ρ[c a (bV )1−a/b − bV ]/a, the same homothetic substitution gives
18                                                                                                 18
                                     ρ

                                  0 = [m a K −a/b − 1] + R∗ − m.                                   19

20
                                     a                                                             20

21   Since f c = VX   implies ρm a−1 K −a/b = 1, the first term becomes (m − ρ)/a. Conse-          21

22   quently, m = ψρ + (1 − ψ) R∗     and K = [ρm a−1 ]b/a . The formula assumes the station-      22

23   ary candidate has positive consumption. It is not an infinite-horizon utility-selection       23

24   theorem.                                                                                      24

25     For the finite-horizon matching-bequest test at a > 0 > b, define Y = bV. The               25

26   consumption domain is compact and bounded away from zero. At sufficiently small               26

27   positive Y, c a Y −a/b < 1 uniformly in consumption, so f < 0. At sufficiently large          27

28   Y, the reverse inequality holds and f > 0. Because dividing by negative b reverses            28

29   the order of the utility endpoints, the large-Y constant is a lower utility barrier and       29

30   the small-Y constant is an upper utility barrier. The matching bequest lies between           30

31   them after enlarging the interval. Lipschitz scalar comparison then keeps each                31

32   policy utility inside the interval, on which the aggregator and its utility derivative        32
     112

1    are bounded. This verifies the finite-horizon interpretation without claiming an           1

2    unrestricted infinite-horizon result at those parameters.                                  2

5                              I.10. Endogenous-Preference Details                              5

6                                                                                               6
     I.10.1. State restrictions and the reference experiment
7                                                                                               7

8      In the baseline application, the risk-aversion interval lies strictly above one and      8

9    the process is reflected. The reference wealth problem is stopped at positive finite       9

10   bounds. Consumption shares are bounded away from zero, adjustment efforts are              10

11   bounded, and the horizon is finite. These choices bound the flow utility, its relevant     11

12   derivatives away from the terminal-boundary compatibility issue, and the bequest. 12
13   They provide a well-defined finite reference calculation. The numerical reflection         13

14   is a folded reflection of Brownian cubature endpoints; the wealth boundary is              14

15   absorbing with the prescribed payoff. The same terminal and boundary values are            15

16   imposed for both adjustment-cost parameters.                                               16

17     The grid reference operates in y = log X. Its drift is                                   17

18                                                                                              18

19                                                                                              19
                                by = r + p(µ − r ) − m − 12 p2 σX2 ,
20                                                                                              20

21                                                                                              21

22   and its covariance with u is ϱσu pσX . The two Brownian-factor columns can be 22
     written (σu , ϱpσX )⊤ and (0, 1 − ϱ2 pσX )⊤ . Four symmetric cubature directions re- 23
                                  p
23

24   produce this covariance. The correction − p2 σX2 /2 in log wealth is essential; omitting   24

25   it would change the economic diffusion.                                                    25

26     For each action, the backward update is current utility times the time step plus         26

27   the exponentially discounted positive-weight interpolation of next-period value. 27
28   Feasible actions are enumerated on the declared 27-element grid. Because the               28

29   terminal/exit payoff is fixed, raising k weakly lowers each action’s payoff and, by        29

30   backward induction, lowers value at every state and time. The recorded arrays              30

31   permit a direct check of this order. The reported grid-refinement difference is not an     31

32   estimate of unrestricted neural approximation error.                                       32
                                          NBO SUPPLEMENT                                   113

1    I.10.2. Policy conditions and comparative statics                                            1

2                                                                                                 2
       The consumption marginal utility in reference units is Uc = c̄−1 (c/c̄)−u . Equating
3                                                                                                 3
     it to VX yields c = c̄(c̄VX )−1/u . Adjustment maximizes θVu − kθ 2 /2 and hence
4                                                                                                 4
     uses the projection of Vu /k under a box. Portfolio choice maximizes a quadratic
5                                                                                                 5
     with coefficient X 2 σX2 VXX /2. The projected first-order solution is valid only when
6                                                                                                 6
     this coefficient is negative. These conditions clarify why numerical curvature and
7                                                                                                 7
     feasibility must be checked independently.
8                                                                                                 8
       The cross derivative VuX is an endogenous object. Its sign cannot be inferred from
9                                                                                                 9
     the label “risk aversion” or from a negative shock correlation. Nor can the sign of
10                                                                                                10
     ∂k θ k be inferred by differentiating 1/k while keeping Vuk fixed. In contrast, the value
11                                                                                                11
     monotonicity in k holds policy by policy. At a differentiable optimum the envelope
12                                                                                                12
     formula follows because k appears only in the quadratic running cost. Changing
13                                                                                                13
     the normalization function κ (u) can change both adjustment and portfolio policies
14                                                                                                14
     even while leaving utility curvature in consumption unchanged.
15                                                                                                15
       An observation model is needed for structural estimation. With latent u, uncer-
16                                                                                                16
     tainty in κ, and unknown adjustment or shock parameters, a sensitivity plot for
17                                                                                                17
     one policy component does not prove identification. A practical estimation exercise
18                                                                                                18
     would compute moment derivatives jointly, inspect their rank after normalization
19                                                                                                19
     restrictions, and propagate numerical approximation error into the implied moment
20                                                                                                20
     map. Those are distinct tasks from solving the controlled economy.
21                                                                                                21

22                                                                                                22
                                  I.11. Temporal-Self Deviations
23                                                                                                23

24     For the two-exponential kernel, the component value under constant consump- 24
25   tion share m is Wj ( X ) = mb X b /[bλ j (m)], where λ j (m) = ρ j − b(r − m). A deviation   25

26   to share z for duration ϵ, followed by m, has component value at X = 1                       26

27                                     "                                     #                    27
                                     1 b 1 − e−λ j (z)ϵ                m  b
28                       Jj (z, ϵ) =     z              + e−λ j (z)ϵ           .                  28
                                     b      λ j (z)                  λ j (m)
29                                                                                                29

30   The continuous extension is used if λ j (z) = 0. The deviation gain is ∑ j w j Jj (z, ϵ) −   30

31   ∑ j w j Wj (1), with weights ( β, 1 − β). This is the quantity independently maximized 31
32   in the experiment, rather than a restatement of the equilibrium first-order condition. 32
     114

1      At an equilibrium share, the first-order gain is nonpositive for every feasible          1

2    instantaneous share and equals zero at the equilibrium share. A finite-duration self       2

3    can generally choose a slightly different share, so the maximized gain need not be         3

4    exactly zero for positive duration. The required diagnostic is its gain divided by         4

5    duration as duration tends to zero. For β = 1 the ordinary exponential solution is         5

6    recovered.                                                                                 6

7      As κ grows, the kernel becomes increasingly concentrated near the present before         7

8    approaching its long-run component. Taking the instantaneous-deviation limit               8

9    and taking κ → ∞ are conceptually different operations. The finite-κ model in this         9

10   paper explicitly specifies the order: the spike condition is derived for fixed κ, and      10

11   any duration-limit comparative static is taken afterward. This avoids assigning an         11

12   undefined continuous-time meaning to a discrete present-bias multiplier.                   12

13                                                                                              13

14                                                                                              14
                          I.12. Games and Independent Best Responses
15                                                                                              15

16     A fixed rival profile turns each player’s problem into an ordinary controlled            16

17   Markov problem, so the main verification results apply player by player. The               17

18   derivative graph must implement this reduction. During player i’s actor step, every        18

19   rival policy and every derivative of player i’s critic is detached. During player i’s      19

20   critic step, all players’ actions are detached. A sum of these reporting losses can be     20

21   logged, but differentiation of a joint-profit objective is not equivalent to those block   21

22   steps.                                                                                     22

23     The two-firm static diagnostic has best response qi = (1 − q j )/2 when interior. At     23

24   the joint-profit allocation (1/4, 1/4), the deviating firm chooses 3/8 and improves        24

25   profit from 1/8 to 9/64, a gain of 1/64. The own-action derivative is 1/4 at the           25

26   joint optimum. These numbers verify a graph-level distinction; they are not a              26

27   computation of the dynamic capital game.                                                   27

28     For the dynamic problem, a reported equilibrium should include an indepen- 28
29   dent solution of each fixed-rival best-response problem, or an upper bound on              29

30   the unilateral Hamiltonian gap over the entire verification domain. A finite list of       30

31   unsuccessful deviations does not supply such an upper bound. Multiple initializa- 31
32   tions, asymmetric profiles, boundary actions, and the market-size normalization are        32
                                        NBO SUPPLEMENT                                    115

1    part of the same experiment. The specification in the main text retains the dynamic        1

2    Cournot application but does not invent a dynamic Nash table from the static               2

3    arithmetic.                                                                                3

4                                                                                               4

5              I.13. Proof and Implementation of the Continuous-Action Envelope                 5

6      We use the notation of the main text’s budget-safe envelope proposition. The             6

7    nodal economy is fixed before verification. Its terminal and stopping payoffs, prim- 7
8    itive coefficients, interpolation abscissae, and compact action box are part of the        8

9    object being bounded. This is stronger than checking many action samples and               9

10   weaker than verifying the continuous-state diffusion without a discretization ac- 10
11   count.                                                                                     11

12                                                                                              12
     I.13.1. Complete-cover invariant and backward proof
13                                                                                              13

14     At a given state and time, the initial action box covers the entire feasible set. Bisec- 14
15   tion replaces one closed box by two closed boxes with the same union. Their shared         15

16   face is harmless. Induction on the number of subdivisions preserves coverage. A            16

17   pruned box is retained logically through the inequality u B ≤ ci + τ, where u B is         17

18   an outward upper enclosure on that whole box, and ci is a lower enclosure at a             18

19   feasible incumbent. If the incumbent later increases, the previous pruning inequality      19

20   remains true. Consequently every feasible action is either in an unresolved box or         20

21   in a pruned box with payoff at most the current threshold. Taking the maximum of           21

22   the threshold and all unresolved upper bounds dominates the action supremum,               22

23   whether or not the supremum is attained. Termination on a numerical budget does            23

24   not change this argument.                                                                  24

25      At N, the terminal inequalities are given. If Vn∗+1 ≤ Un+1 , positivity gives 25
26   Tn Vn∗+1 ≤ Tn Un+1 ≤ Un . If Ln+1 ≤ Vnπ+1 , the same argument gives Ln ≤ Tnπn Ln+1 ≤ 26
27   Tnπn Vnπ+1 = Vnπ . Feasibility implies Vnπ ≤ Vn∗ . This proves the main envelope inequal- 27
28   ity. Substochasticity further gives                                                        28

29                                                                                              29
                                 ∥ Tn w − Tn z∥∞ ≤ qn ∥w − z∥∞ .
30                                                                                              30

31   Applying this inequality to Un − Tn Vn∗+1 yields the stated excess recursion and its       31

32   product-sum expansion. The proof uses qn > 0, not a stationary contraction assump- 32
     116

1    tion. Outward addition is used for the pruning threshold and final subtraction. The      1

2    implementation records a requested tolerance and a retained optimization bracket         2

3    separately. A budget-exhausted bracket is never replaced by that tolerance. Q.E.D.       3

4                                                                                             4

5
     I.13.2. Interpolation, reflection, stopping, and arithmetic                              5

6      For a cell with coordinates (u0 , u1 ) × (y0 , y1 ), the continuation is               6

7                                                                                             7
               Iw(u, y) = (1 − s)(1 − t)w00 + s(1 − t)w10 + (1 − s)tw01 + stw11 ,
8                                                                                             8
                                       u − u0          y − y0
9                                   s=         ,    t=         .                              9
                                       u1 − u0         y1 − y0
10                                                                                            10

11   For fixed u, this is affine in y, so its extremes on a subrectangle occur at the two y   11

12   endpoints. At either endpoint it is affine in u, so the extremes occur at the two u end- 12
13   points. Applying this argument to every intersected cell proves the corner enclosure. 13
14   Nonnegative interpolation weights also permit interval nodal inputs: evaluating the      14

15   corner expressions outward encloses every admissible nodal realization. A separate       15

16   valid enclosure evaluates the rectangle center and adds cell-slope bounds over every     16

17   intersected cell. Slopes are computed from stored nodal differences, not estimated       17

18   from random derivative samples.                                                          18

19     Preference reflection is piecewise affine. Its extrema on an interval occur at the     19

20   interval endpoints or a crossed folding point. The allowed one-step movement is          20

21   checked to exclude unhandled multiple folds. For log wealth, boxes fully inside          21

22   the domain use interpolation, boxes fully outside use the prescribed clipped exit        22

23   payoff, and boxes crossing an exit face use the union of both. On the stored grid the    23

24   boundary nodes equal the exact prescribed payoff; at an off-grid exit the analytic       24

25   payoff need not equal its nodal interpolant. The union construction avoids an            25

26   unjustified global smoothness or Lipschitz assumption across that switch.                26

27     The implementation inherits the archived outward elementary and transcen- 27
28   dental routines. Products, quotients, powers through logarithm and exponential,          28

29   and endpoint rounding are enclosed. The logarithm’s input is bounded below by            29

30   0.02; the preference denominator is separated from zero because u ∈ [1.2, 3]. The        30

31   recorded control model treats the stored binary64 primitive coefficients and ab- 31
32   scissae as exact real inputs. The evidence also tests agreement with the inherited       32
                                         NBO SUPPLEMENT                                       117

1    training evaluator on the finite action grid. Such a regression check is useful for            1

2    implementation consistency but is not the proof of global action coverage. That                2

3    proof is the maintained union invariant and the interval inclusion property. The               3

4    arithmetic account is conditional on the specified floating-point execution model,             4

5    not a formal verification of hardware, libraries, or the Python interpreter.                   5

6                                                                                                   6

7    I.13.3. A common upper envelope for multiple policies                                          7

8      The optimal upper array depends on the economy, not on which feasible incum- 8
9    bent produced the lower search values. Thus the completed upper array can be                   9

10   reused for any frozen feasible policy on the same state, time, and continuous-action           10

11   domains. We separately propagate each policy’s lower and upper payoff arrays and               11

12   subtract its lower array from the common optimal upper array. Costs are reported               12

13   both as shared verification and as the cost of an individual policy evaluation. Shar- 13
14   ing a verifier does not make its cost vanish. It also does not justify transferring the        14

15   upper array to a changed wealth box, action constraint, utility normalization, time            15

16   step, or interpolation rule.                                                                   16

17                                                                                                  17

18                                                                                                  18
                   I.14. Separating Numerical and Continuous-Economy Error
19                                                                                                  19

20
       The following result specifies the transfer work needed in addition to a nodal cer- 20
21
     tificate. It is not used to set an uncomputed transfer term to zero. Let Tn be the exact       21

22
     economic one-step optimality operator with its continuously monitored stopping                 22

23
     and reflection rule, and let Tnπ be the operator for a declared continuous-state im- 23
24
     plementation of the numerical policy. Let In be a positive, sup-norm-nonexpansive              24

25
     interpolation from grid values to the economic state domain. Suppose the exact                 25

26
     operators are qn -Lipschitz on the comparison class.                                           26

14     The proposition can use local regularity, coupling, barrier, or weak-approximation                  14

15   estimates to bound its defects; it does not claim that those hypotheses hold au- 15
16   tomatically for a fitted critic near a stopped boundary. In the present two-state                     16

17   computations, action coverage is operationally bounded on the nodal economy. 17
18   The continuous-state/time defects remain explicitly uncomputed. Nested grids                          18

19   and common frozen policies diagnose sensitivity but do not provide the one-sided                      19

20   inequalities required by this proposition.                                                            20

                         I.15. Strategic Deviations and Missing Pure Stages
19                                                                                              19

20     For a fixed Markov rival profile in a finite-horizon finite-state/action game, condi- 20
21   tion on any history. The own player’s conditional payoff from a randomized current         21

22   action is a convex combination of pure-action payoffs with the same continuation           22

23   best-response values. It cannot exceed the largest such payoff. Backward induction         23

24   therefore bounds every randomized and history-dependent unilateral policy by the           24

25   Markov pure-action best-response recursion. Outward propagation then provides              25

26   a bound over this full deviation class, not merely the particular local deviations         26

27   observed during actor training.                                                            27

28     Existence of a pure equilibrium in a fitted one-step game is a different matter. 28
29   If none exists, minimum-defect selection produces a feasible profile, not an exact         29

30   equilibrium. The independent dynamic best response still evaluates that profile            30

31   correctly. The following records give every missing pure stage in the six principal        31

32   finite-policy study neural runs, including time index, state, and minimum maximum          32
     120

1    stage defect. Indices begin at zero. The six runs have 6 × 30 × 225 = 40,500 active                      1

2    state-time nodes; the five recorded exceptions are not excluded from their final                         2

3    dynamic bounds.                                                                                          3

           APPENDIX J: S TOCHASTIC H ESSIAN O BJECTIVES AND C OMPLEXITY                         7

8                                                                                               8
       Let B = Σ⊤ D2 vΣ, a symmetric matrix in Brownian-factor space. For Gaussian
9                                                                                               9
     probes, Isserlis’ identity gives E(z⊤ Bz) = tr B and Var(z⊤ Bz) = 2 tr( B2 ) = 2∥ B∥2F .
10                                                                                              10
     Averaging K independent probes divides the variance by K. Rademacher probes
11                                                                                              11
     instead have variance 2 ∑i̸= j Bij2 /K. The variance depends on the chosen covariance
12                                                                                              12
     factor and probe law, both of which must be specified.
13                                                                                              13
       Write qbj = q + ϵ j , with conditionally mean-zero independent errors. Then
14                                                                                              14

15                                                                                              15
               E[(d − qb1 /2)(d − qb2 /2)] = (d − q/2)2 + 14 E[ϵ1 ϵ2 ] = (d − q/2)2 .
16                                                                                              16

17                                                                                              17

18   Using one bank twice would retain its variance term. In the scalar Gaussian example,       18

19   a single-bank squared residual has expectation 1 − h + 3h2 /4, minimized at h = 2/3,       19

20   whereas the exact residual (1 − h/2)2 is minimized at h = 2. The independent               20

21   product restores the latter population objective. The saved Monte Carlo diagnostic         21

22   uses 400,000 pairs and records both empirical estimates; it does not claim that the        22

23   sampling error is identically zero.                                                        23

24     The core implementation uses exact Hessians to make the covariance contraction           24

25   independently testable. A Hessian-vector implementation can replace it without             25

26   changing the conditional-independence requirement. The cost of a Hessian-vector            26

27   product depends on the network graph, not just the state dimension. Total cost also        27

28   includes sampling, optimization, domain coverage, action maximization, and stop- 28
29   ping at an agreed error level. A quadratic-neural representation with a Lyapunov           29

30   solve has different approximation and optimization costs from a generic multilayer         30

31   network. The quadratic laboratory is explicitly the former; the new dense nonlinear        31

32   experiments actually train generic networks and Hessian-vector estimators.                 32
                                         NBO SUPPLEMENT                                      125

1                        J.1. Implemented Error Accounts and Arithmetic                            1

2                                                                                                  2
       The implemented map returns a candidate and a verification record, rather than
3                                                                                                  3
     assigning authority to an optimizer’s exit code. In the differential consumption
4                                                                                                  4
     economy, the record identifies the binary64 weights, both stopping faces, all dyadic
5                                                                                                  5
     leaves, global consumption maximization, and the error bounds for the continuous
6                                                                                                  6
     diffusion. In NDU it instead identifies the finite state–action–time model, its positive
7                                                                                                  7
     transition weights, and time-indexed evaluation and improvement errors. The
8                                                                                                  8
     dynamic-game record fixes the entire rival policy before an independent backward
9                                                                                                  9
     best-response solve. An unchecked continuous-action or time-discretization term is
10                                                                                                 10
     represented as unavailable in the machine-readable output, not as zero.
11                                                                                                 11
       For finite time levels, let δt = ∥vt − Tta vt+1 ∥∞ and ϵt = ∥ Tt vt+1 − Tta vt+1 ∥∞ . If
12                                                                                                 12
     both maps are β t -Lipschitz, the triangle inequality gives
13                                                                                                 13

14                                                                                                 14

15     ∥vt − Vta ∥ ≤ δt + β t ∥vt+1 − Vta+1 ∥,    ∥vt − Vt∗ ∥ ≤ δt + ϵt + β t ∥vt+1 − Vt∗+1 ∥.     15

16                                                                                                 16

17   Summing the two inequalities yields the regret account. The same recurrence with              17

18   a player’s unilateral gap bounds the gain from a dynamic deviation when that                  18

19   player’s policy evaluation is exact. Accounts must be checked at each time level:             19

20   a time-zero bound need not dominate a later-time bound when discounting and                   20

21   errors differ. The coarse-game regression exercises this distinction and verifies that        21

22   a missing pure equilibrium is reported rather than silently treated as exact.                 22

23                                                                                                 23

24                                                                                                 24
     J.1.1. Full-domain interval implementation
25                                                                                                 25

26     An interval [l, u] encloses an exact real quantity. Each elementary operation ex- 26
27   pands its rounded endpoints with the adjacent binary64 number. Affine layers split            27

28   positive and negative weights and include a conservative dot-product forward-error            28

29   allowance. The arithmetic contract requires binary64 round-to-nearest arithmetic,             29

30   the stated dot-product error bound, and absence of exceptional overflow or flush- 30
31   to-zero behavior. Stored parameters denote their exact binary64 real values; the              31

32   verifier does not assert an unrecorded decimal model identity.                                32
     126

1      For an exponential argument x, range reduction gives r = x/2s with |r | ≤ 1/8. A      1

2    degree-fourteen Taylor polynomial has remainder bounded by                              2

3                                                                                            3
                               e|r| |r |15 /15! < 2(1/8)15 /15! < 2−80 .
4                                                                                            4

5    Interval Horner evaluation and repeated squaring enclose e x . The identity tanh x =    5

6    1 − 2/(e2x + 1) then supplies tanh values and derivative channels 1 − tanh2 x and       6

7    −2 tanh x (1 − tanh2 x ). Powers of two and the range-reduction domain are checked      7

8    in the code.                                                                            8

9      For logarithms, exact binary decomposition gives x = 2k m, with 1 ≤ m < 2. Writ- 9
10   ing z = (m − 1)/(m + 1),                                                                10

11                                                                                           11
                                24
                                    z2j+1                      2|z|51
12                   log m = 2 ∑           + R,       | R| ≤          2
                                                                        < 2−72               12
                               j =0
                                    2j + 1                   51(1 − z )
13                                                                                           13

14   for the outward-enclosed range |z| < 0.334. The same expansion encloses log 2. Nei- 14
15   ther construction presumes correctly rounded library tanh or logarithm functions. 15
16   The saved test suite checks transcendental endpoints against eighty-digit arithmetic,   16

17   differentiates the trained network independently, and checks all certificate hashes     17

18   and leaf adjacencies. These tests supplement, but do not replace, the arithmetic        18

19   contract.                                                                               19

20     The scalar action objective h(c) = log c − pc has second derivative −1/c2 . If C      20

21   encloses both the actor and its maximizing alternative, strong concavity on their       21

22   joining segment gives                                                                   22

23                                                                                           23
                 h(c∗ ) − h(c) ≤ h′ (c)(c∗ − c) − (c∗ − c)2 /(2C2 ) ≤ C2 h′ (c)2 /2.
24                                                                                           24

25   The argument includes constrained endpoints. Taking the minimum with the natu- 25
26   ral interval action-gap enclosure markedly reduces verification cost, without using     26

27   a sampled gradient as a bound. Residual targets are decided on every leaf; reaching     27

28   a maximum subdivision depth does not change a failed target into a pass.                28

29                                                                                           29

30
     J.1.2. Application-specific hypotheses                                                  30

31     The stopped consumption diffusion is uniformly nondegenerate on its compact           31

32   domain; controls and coefficients are bounded and its endpoint data are continuous. 32
                                       NBO SUPPLEMENT                                  127

1    The smooth networks and strictly positive consumption map meet the regularity            1

2    and log-domain requirements of the verification argument. Both stopping-face             2

3    errors and the full action interval are checked. The upwind reference error is not a     3

4    premise of its continuous certificate.                                                   4

5      The NDU computation is a positive-weight, finite-action, finite-horizon economy        5

6    with prescribed discrete stopping and folded preference transitions. Positivity,         6

7    all terminal and wealth-face values, and every finite action are evaluated. The          7

8    continuous reflected PDE’s corner compatibility, continuous-action coverage error,       8

9    and diffusion approximation error are not inferred from these tests. Expanding a         9

10   feasible box changes the constrained economy; densifying a fixed box does not. The       10

11   sensitivity record keeps these operations separate.                                      11

12     The dense capital computation uses bounded nonlinear drift in log coordinates,         12

13   bounded positive consumption fractions, constant diffusion, and finite horizon. Its      13

14   flow has at most linear log-state growth and its terminal payoff at most quadratic       14

15   growth; these are integrable under the declared bounded-drift diffusion. The fitted      15

16   box is a sampling domain, not a state constraint. Its sampled differential residuals     16

17   do not check comparison barriers or uniform errors on Rd . Euler payoff estimates        17

18   carry both sampling error and unbounded-state/time-discretization issues not             18

19   covered by the reported Monte Carlo standard error.                                      19

20     The dynamic capital game is a fully enumerated finite approximation with               20

21   stopped joint boundaries. Stage equilibria and dynamic fixed-rival best responses        21

22   are numerical statements about that model. The continuous-control best-response          22

23   comparison assumptions in the main theorem remain logically distinct. The re- 23
24   tained homothetic recursive-utility and temporal-self examples use their stated          24

25   utility domains, bequests, and integrability conditions; initial evaluation study does   25

26   not relabel them as unrestricted neural experiments.                                     26

                      K.1. Observation and the Economic Implementation
30                                                                                            30

31     The capital state and the innovation-driven numerical controller are distinct. We      31

32   now specify an observation model under which the controller uses the history of          32
     128

1    observed capital rather than latent economic shocks. The model assumes contin- 1
2    uous noiseless observation of every capital coordinate, known diffusion and drift              2

3    coefficients, knowledge of the controller’s own actions, and independent private               3

4    randomization. It does not assume that a finite set of noisy observations reveals              4

5    Brownian increments.                                                                           5

20   cluding the componentwise clipping rule, yields the same closed-loop law and payoff as         20

22   statements apply under this observation model.                                                 22

23                                                                                                  23

24     Private randomization in (S.112) is essential for this exact equivalence: the right- 24
25   inverse term alone has rank d, whereas the implemented clipping acts separately                25

26   on d + 1 drivers. The construction does not recover the original unobservable null             26

27   component path by path. It replaces that component by an independent one with                  27

28   the correct law. Nor is the resulting controller a deterministic function of the state at      28

29   a single decision date; its memory and independent randomization are part of its               29

30   definition. The integrated drift in the proposition is an observation-model primitive. 30
31   A sensor grid, drift quadrature or measurement error would need a further transfer             31

32   allowance and is not silently treated as exact.                                                32

19     This argument also establishes causality. At a decision date, the already observed         19

20   drift integral determines the preceding innovation increment and hence the next              20

21   internal state. The next action is then fixed before the next Brownian increment. The        21

22   held-action state equation is uniquely solvable on that cell. Induction identifies the       22

23   joint law of physical state, internal state, actions, and recovered innovations with         23

24   the prescribed innovation controller. The independent null component supplies the            24

25   correct law when clipping is applied separately to d + 1 coordinates; it does not            25

26   recover the original latent null component. The exact observation result is separate         26

27   from the finite measurement implementation below.                                            27

       APPENDIX L: C OMPUTATIONAL D ESIGN AND THE E XISTING C OMPARISON
21                                                                                             21

22     The numerical question is whether the learned evaluation block improves the             22

23   economic accuracy or complete work of the NBO map. All comparisons identify the           23

24   economic model, feasible actions, evaluation target, selected policy, final payoff law,   24

25   and work boundaries. A policy-specific confidence interval conditions on its fitted       25

26   objects. A statement about a training method additionally specifies the distribution      26

27   of training randomness and its sampling uncertainty. An interval containing zero          27

28   establishes neither superiority nor practical equivalence.                                28

29                                                                                             29

30
                              L.1. The executed capital comparison                             30

31     The source-bound reference study contains ninety primary fitted policies in             31

32   dimensions ten, twenty, and fifty. Its primary fitting design uses an announced           32
     136

1    twenty-second budget, completes the current iteration, and selects the checkpoint                                         1

2    using validation data. Each final primary row uses 8,192 independent paths and                                            2

3    1,024 time cells. The comparison concerns a fixed nine-profile population and a                                           3

4    separate origin calculation. The simultaneous inference construction covers the                                           4

5    declared finite family of selected policies and profiles.                                                                 5

6       Every fitted policy in the reported primary schedule comparisons has a positive                                        6

7    simultaneous lower gain endpoint. For the prespecified population, all thirty NBO                                         7

8    policies have this property. These are improvements over the analytical schedule;                                         8

9    the direct method comparisons have a different conclusion. Table L.I retains the                                          9

10   findings that motivate the current evaluation experiment.                                                                 10

11                                                                                                                             11
                                                         TABLE L.I

     continuous-time candidate-occupation welfare bound. The complete original rows, arrays, work measurements and

29      The negative sample means do not prove that Raw dominates NBO, because the                                             29

30   simultaneous direct intervals remain inconclusive. The mechanism comparisons                                              30

31   nevertheless provide no positive evidence for the fitted critic in that design. They                                      31

32   cannot be converted into a favorable mechanism result by observing that the com- 32
                                       NBO SUPPLEMENT                                 137

1    mon verifier accepts NBO policies. Likewise, the fixed-work experiment matches          1

2    simulator visits and checkpoints but has different optimizer update counts; it does     2

3    not establish equal total computation.                                                  3

4                                                                                            4

5                                                                                            5
                            L.2. Accuracy objects and economic scale
6                                                                                            6

7      The original finite-population NBO regret upper bounds are approximately              7

8    0.02097, 0.02847 and 0.03039 in dimensions ten, twenty and fifty. The corresponding     8

9    mean schedule improvements are approximately 0.00147, 0.00135 and 0.00161. The          9

10   regret bounds combine the analytical anchor gap with the measured improvement. 10
11   They are reported as absolute upper bounds, without interpreting them as tight          11

12   high-dimensional HJB errors.                                                            12

13     The independent scalar computation uses a monotone implicit HJB scheme and            13

14   full-box action maximization on four original grids. At initial log capital zero, the   14

15   finest domain-six NBO loss is about 3.44 × 10−6 , compared with 1.16 × 10−4 for         15

16   direct policy optimization. At initial state one, the corresponding losses are about    16

17   1.05 × 10−4 and 5.02 × 10−5 . The ordering is therefore state dependent. These are      17

18   finite-grid losses of Markov feedbacks in the scalar model; the widening experiment     18

19   changes both domain and spatial spacing. The residual, refinement and five-state        19

20   accounts remain in Appendix M.                                                          20

21     The balance-sheet profiles and the resource fee in (S.17) are transparent model       21

22   experiments. They are not an empirical calibration. The fee transformation reports      22

23   what administration cost a verified utility gain can support under the specified        23

24   log-felicity resource account. It does not provide an additional independent source     24

25   of evidence for the critic.                                                             25

26                                                                                           26

27                                                                                           27
                              L.3. Work and comparison semantics
28                                                                                           28

29     The original time-budget and fixed-work records retain fitting clocks, simu- 29
30   lator visits, optimizer updates, peak process memory, selected checkpoints and          30

31   verification components. Their verification clock begins after weight loading and       31

32   deterministic-constant construction. Their prefix frontier is retrospective. Those      32
     138

1    quantities remain useful descriptions of the original computation, with those bound- 1
2    aries.                                                                                       2

3      The current method-level comparison specifies its training-randomness estimand,            3

4    economic target, actual stopping rule, complete cost boundary, and final inferential         4

5    object before its confirmatory evaluation. It must charge failed checks and retain           5

6    unattained targets. A direct HJB or policy-iteration comparator isolates the role            6

7    of a separately learned actor and continuation object under a shared model and               7

8    verifier. A classical low-dimensional method serves as a policy generator as well as         8

9    an independent reference. The following study gives the concrete protocol and its            9

10   executed results.                                                                            10

11                                                                                                11

12                APPENDIX M: I NDEPENDENT N UMERICAL D IAGNOSTICS                                12

     Discount is ρ = 0.08, flow utility is log c, and exit pays g( x ) = log(0.12 + 0.08x )/0.08. 19
20                                                                                                20
     This is a specified stopped economy, not a claim about an unbounded-wealth
21                                                                                                21
     problem. Income and the exit option destroy the homothetic reduction used in
22                                                                                                22
     the portfolio calibration. Bounded coefficients, a compact positive consumption
23                                                                                                23
     interval, positive diffusion variance on the closed domain, and continuous endpoint
24                                                                                                24

25                                                                                                25

17      Indeed, constant upper and lower shifts of v dominate the evaluation or optimal- 17
18   ity residual and the stopping data; comparison gives the first two inequalities and                    18

19   their sum gives the third. No reference-grid error is used in this argument.                           19

20      The executable verifier covers the entire state interval by dyadic subintervals. 20
21   Outward-rounded propagation through the stored network encloses v, v′ , v′′ , and                      21

22   the actor on every leaf. Equation (S.117) supplies a global action enclosure. We also                  22

23   use the concavity bound                                                                                23

24                                                                                                          24
                 log c∗ − pc∗ − (log c − pc) ≤ 12 C2 (1/c − p)2 ,        C ≥ max{c, c∗ },        (S.119)
25                                                                                                          25

26   which follows by completing the square in the strong-concavity inequality on the                       26

27   segment joining the two actions. It does not assume an interior maximizer. Taking                      27

28   the better of this bound and a direct interval enclosure avoids dependency inflation. 28
29   Terminal-free boundary errors are evaluated separately at both endpoints, including                    29

30   rounding in the lifting constants.                                                                     30

31      The certificate saves every leaf, verifies that their union is exactly [0.5, 2.5], and              31

32   binds the parameter and arithmetic files by hashes. Exponential and logarithmic                        32
     140

1    enclosures use range reduction and polynomial remainder bounds rather than              1

2    an assumed accuracy of a library transcendental. Its mathematical conclusion is         2

3    conditional on the stated binary64 arithmetic contract; high-precision and derivative   3

4    tests check the implementation but are not a formal machine proof. The construction     4

5    does not estimate a global Lipschitz constant from samples.                             5

8                                                                                            8

9    M.1.1. Controlled comparison and failures                                               9

10                                                                                           10

11     We use seeds 11, 29, and 47, each with 256 prespecified jittered collocation points. 11
12   Eight outer rounds have a common cap of 800 L-BFGS inner iterations. NBO allo- 12
13   cates each round sixty iterations to evaluation and forty to improvement; direct        13

14   neural HJB uses the same critic and the global action rule in all one hundred itera- 14
15   tions. A joint-loss ablation differentiates the sum of squared residual and negative    15

16   Hamiltonian through both blocks. Line searches use different numbers of closures,       16

17   so this is a matched iteration cap, not an assertion of equal floating-point work. 17
18   Closure counts and elapsed times are saved.                                             18

19     An independent positive-rate upwind policy-iteration solver uses continuous- 19
20   action maximization within each drift region. Reference grids have 320, 640, 1,280,     20

21   and 2,560 intervals. Value and derivative comparisons use the finest grid; its re- 21
22   finement differences are not substituted for a rigorous discretization bound. The       22

23   continuous certificate instead checks the neural functions directly.                    23

24     All three NBO and direct-HJB runs attain the declared interval-verification thresh- 24
25   olds. The joint-loss ablation does not: a residual near one half is accompanied by      25

26   large value and action errors. Width, depth, and update-allocation variations are       26

27   retained in the supplement, including unfavorable outcomes. The direct method           27

28   is a strong comparator: these results do not establish that carrying an actor is uni- 28
29   formly faster or more accurate. They establish an end-to-end neural solve with an       29

30   independently computable welfare bound, and identify a concrete failure of joint        30

31   differentiation. Fitting and full-domain verification are separately timed because      31

32   their computational burdens are different.                                              32
                                               NBO SUPPLEMENT                                             141

5    The archived propagation diagnostic substitutes the observed floating-point resid- 5

7    substitution is a numerical diagnostic rather than a certified bound. The estimate        7

10     The tables report all four grids, the five initial states y0 ∈ {−1, −0.5, 0, 0.5, 1},   10

11   the policies’ action-bound frequencies, and changes in values, optimal actions and        11

12   policy losses. Initial-state values are linearly interpolated from nodal time-zero        12

13   values. Bound frequencies use an absolute tolerance of 10−12 and count equally            13

14   weighted interior state–time nodes; they are not occupation probabilities. The            14

15   first two grid comparisons halve both h and δ. Increasing L from 6 to 8 at fixed          15

16   (ny , nt ) = (1601, 1024) changes δ from 0.0075 to 0.01. This comparison measures         16

17   joint domain and spacing sensitivity, not boundary truncation error in isolation. 17
18   Full common-domain changes and their locations accompany the central-region               18

19   summaries.                                                                                19

20     The scalar calculation supplies an independent finite-grid benchmark for non- 20
21   linear feedback, with a reproducible algebraic error account. In the 10-, 20- and         21

22   50-dimensional economies, cross-sectional dispersion, the coupling matrix and the         22

23   covariance structure remain economically active. Moreover, this scalar benchmark          23

24   evaluates Markov feedback, whereas the continuous-time deployment theorem                 24

25   specifies a randomized controller with innovation history. Scalar reference losses        25

26   therefore have their stated one-dimensional meaning; multidimensional conclu- 26
27   sions rely on the separate multidimensional policy comparisons and certification          27

28   arguments.                                                                                28

       The experiment fixes the same saved NBO checkpoint before drawing the diag-
3                                                                                                 3
     nostic bank. The sensor uses finite state measurements and independent private
4                                                                                                 4
     noise; the parent uses a clipped-innovation internal state. The deterministic al-
5                                                                                                 5
     lowance and the finite-Euler measurement answer different questions. Neither the

12   APPENDIX P: A N O UTWARD A LGEBRAIC E RROR A CCOUNT FOR THE S CALAR                                        12

13                                             C OMPARATOR                                                      13

14     Fix the declared spatial nodes, common spacing ∆y, time step h, action endpoints,                        14

15   and terminal and boundary data. Each stored binary64 input denotes an exact real                           15

16   in this finite-grid problem. Let η = (σI2 + σC2 )/2, d0 (y) = a − η + κ tanh y, and define                 16

17   the forward and backward slopes p f = (vi+1 − vi )/∆y and pb = (vi − vi−1 )/∆y. 17
18   The action-dependent upwind Hamiltonian is                                                                 18

19                                                                                                              19
                         ζ
20    Hi (m; v) = log m − m2 + max{d0 (yi ) − m, 0} p f + min{d0 (yi ) − m, 0} pb . (S.120)                     20
                         2
21                                                                                                              21

22   The positive-drift and negative-drift branches are each concave in m. Their fea- 22
23   sible intervals are, respectively, [ℓ, min{u, d0 (yi )}] and [max{ℓ, d0 (yi )}, u], when                   23

24   nonempty. On either branch, the unconstrained positive critical point solves                               24

25   1/m − ζm = p j . The Howard update clips this point to the branch interval and                             25

26   compares the two resulting objective values. Thus the sign of the drift selects the                        26

27   appropriate difference operator throughout maximization.                                                   27

28     The floating-point Howard solve proposes values and actions. A separate out- 28
29   ward calculation encloses the Bellman residual, including the error in the proposed                        29

30   maximizer. For a concave branch H on [ a, b] and any m◦ ∈ [ a, b],                                         30

31                                                                                                              31
           max H (m) ≤ H (m◦ ) + max{0, H ′ (m◦ )( a − m◦ ), H ′ (m◦ )(b − m◦ )}.                     (S.121)
32        m∈[ a,b]                                                                                              32

1    Interval evaluations of log, tanh, the slopes, and the arithmetic operations enclose             1

2    the right-hand side. Interval uncertainty in d0 (yi ) is handled by enlarging each               2

3    branch interval before constructing its upper bound. An arbitrary globally feasible              3

4    action supplies a lower bound for the maximized Hamiltonian. Combining the two                   4

5    bounds encloses                                                                                  5

29   Both rates are nonnegative. Each frozen-policy matrix therefore has nonpositive                  29

30   off-diagonal entries and diagonal dominance at least h−1 + ρ after the boundary                  30

31   values are moved to the right-hand side. This is the M-matrix comparison property                31

32   used below.                                                                                      32

1      For completeness, existence can be established at each backward step by uni- 1
2    formizing these finitely many bounded rates. Choose Λ ≥ maxi,m {λi− (m) + λi+ (m)}. 2
3    When Λ > 0, Lm = Λ( Pm − I ), where Pm has nonnegative entries and row sums                             3

4    one before the fixed boundary values are substituted. The Bellman equation is                           4

5    a fixed-point equation whose dependence on the unknown interior values has                              5

6    supremum-norm Lipschitz constant at most Λ/(h−1 + ρ + Λ) < 1. The fixed-point                           6

7    theorem gives a unique solution. If Λ = 0, the equation is pointwise and its co- 7
8    efficient h−1 + ρ is strictly positive. Backward recursion starts from the specified                    8

9    terminal values.                                                                                        9

10     To establish the error bound, suppose ∥vk+1 − vek+1 ∥∞ ≤ Ek+1 and set ei = vk,i −                     10

11   vek,i . If M = maxi ei > 0, the maximum occurs at an interior node i, because the                       11

12   boundary error is zero. Nonnegative rates give ( Lm e)i ≤ 0 for every feasible m. 12
13   Writing f i (m) = yi + log m − ζm2 /2, it follows that                                                  13

14                                                                                                           14
                         sup{( Lm vk )i + f i (m)} − sup{( Lm vek )i + f i (m)} ≤ 0.

8    tone in continuation and current interior values. Enlarging the maximization set                  8

9    increases the map pointwise. The contraction fixed points therefore inherit this                  9
